Foreword
A PAR T from laying the foundations of demography in this subcontinent, a
hundred years of the Indian Census has also produced elaborate and scholarly accounts of the variegated phenomena of Indian life-sometimes with no
statistics attackea, but usually with just enough statistics to give empirical
underpinning to their conclusions. In a country, largely illiterate, where statistical or numerical comprehension of even such a simple thing as age was
liable to be inaccurate, an understanding of the social structure was essential. It was more necessary to attain a broad understanding of what was happening around oneself than to wrap oneself up in 'statistical ingenuity' or
'mathematical manipulation'. This explains why the Indian Census came to be
interested in 'many by paths' and 'nearly every branch of scholarship, from
anthropology and sociology to geography and religion'.
In the last few decades the Census has increasingly turned its efforts to
the presentation of village statistics. This suits the temper of the times as
well as our political and economic structure. For even as we have a great
deal of centralization on the one hand and decentralisation on the other, my
colleagues thought it would be a welcome continuation of the Census tradition to try t6 invest the dry bones of village statistics with flesh-and-blood accounts of social structure and social change. It was accordingly decided to
select a few villages in every State for special study, where personal observation would be brought to bear on the interpretation of statistics to find out
how much of a village was static and yet changing and how fast the winds of
change were blowing and from where.
Randomness of selection was, therefore, eschewed. There was no intention to build up a picture for the whole State in quantitative terms on the
basis of villages selected statistically at random. The selection was avowedly purposive: the object being as much to find out what was happening and
how fast to those villages which had fewer reasons to choose change and
more to remain lodged in the past as to discover how the more 'normal' types
of villages were changing. They were to be primarily type studies which, by
Virtue of their number and distribution, would also give the reader a 'feel'
of what was going OIl and some kind of a map of the country.
A brief account of the tests of selection will help to explain. A minimum
()f thirty-five villages was to be chosen with great ca~e to represent adequately geopraghicaJ, occupational and even ethnic diversity. Of this minimum
()f thirty-five, the distribution was to be as follows:
(a) At least eight villages were to be so selected that each of them would
<:ontain one dominant community with one predominating occupation, e.g.,
fishermen, forest workers, jhum cultivators, potters, weavers, salt-makers,
quarry workers etc. A village should have a minimum popUlation of 400, the
()ptimum being between 500 and 700.

IV
(b) At least seven villages were to be
of numerically prominent Scheduled Tribes
of the State. Each village could represent a
particular tribe. The minimum population
should be 400 the optimum being between
500 and 700.
(c) The third group of villages should
each be of fair size, of an old and settled
character and contain variegated occupations
and be, if possible, multi-ethnic in composition. By fair size was meant a population of
500-700 persons or more. The village should
mainly depend on agriculture and be sufficiently away from the major sources of
modern communication such as the district
administrative headquarters and business
centres. It should be roughly a day's journey·
from the above places. The villages were to
be selected with an eye to variation in terms
of size, proximity to city and other means of
modern communication, nearness to hills,
jungles and major rivers. Thus there was to
be a regional distribution throughout the
State of this category of villages. If, however, a particular district contained significant
ecological variations within its area, more
than one village in the district might be selected to study the special adjustments to
_them.

It is a unique feature of these village surveys
that they rapidly outgrew their original terms
of reference, as my colleagues warmed up to
their work. This proved for them an absorbing voyage of discovery and their infectio_us
enthusiasm compelled me to enlarge the mquiry's scope again and again. It was just as
well cautiously to feel one's way about at first
and then venture further a field, and although
it accounts to some extent for a certain unevenness in the quality and coverage of the
monographs, it served to compensate the purely honorary and extra-mural rigours of the
task. For, the Survey, along with its many ancillaries like the survey of fairs and festivals,
of small and rural industry and others, was an
'extra', over and above the crushing load of
the 1961 Census.
It might be of interest to recount brie~y
the stages by which the Survey enlarged Its
scope. At the first Census Conference in September 1959, the Survey set itself the task of
what might be called a record in situ of mat~rial traits, like settlement patterns of the vIllage; house types; diet; dress, ornaments and
footweari furnitur~ a,nd storin~ vessels; ~orq-

FOREWORD

mon means of transport of goods and passengers; domestication of animals and birds;
markets attended; worship of deities; festivals
and fairs. There were to be recordings of
course, of cultural and social traits and occupational mobility. This was followed up in
March 1960 by two specimen schedules, one
for each household, the other for the village
as a whole, which, apart from spelling out
the mode of inquiry suggested in the September 1959 conference, introduced groups
of questions aimed at sensing changes in attitude and behaviour in such fields as marriage, inheritance, moveable and immoveable
property, industry, indebtedness, education,
community life and collective activity, social
disabilities, forums of appeal over disputes,
village leadership and organisation of cultural
life. It was now plainly the intention to provide adequate statistical support to empirical
'feel', to approach qualitative change through
statistical quantities. It had been difficult to
give thought to the importance of 'just enough
statistics to give empirical underpinning to
conclusions', at a time when my colleagues
were straining themselves to the utmost for the
success of the main Census operations, but
once the census count itself was left behind in
March 1961, a series of three regional seminars in Trivandrum (May 1961), Darjeeling
and Srinagar (June 1961) restored their attention to this field and the importance of tracing
social change through a number of well-devised statistical tables was once again recognised.
This itself presupposed a fresh survey of villages already done; but it was worth the trouble
in view of the possibilities that a close analysis of statistics and also because the 'consanguinity' schedule remained to be convassed.
By November 1961, however, more was expected of these surveys than ever before.
There was dissatisfaction on the one hand
with too many general statements and a growing desire on the other to draw conclusions
from statistics, to regard social and economic
data as interrelated processes, and finally to
examine the social and economic processes
set in motion through land reforms and other
laws, legislative and administrative measures,
technological and cultural change. In the latter half of 1961 again was organised within
the Census Commission a section on Social
Studies which assumed the task of giving
shape to the general frame of st~dy and providing technical help to Supermtendents of
Census Operations in the matter of cond~ctin~ surveys, their analysis and presentatIon.
o

t,'oRtwORJ)

This section headed by Dr. B. K. Roy Burman has been responsible for going through
each monograph and offering usdul suggestions which were much welcomed by my col·
leagues. Finally, a study camp was organised
in the last week of December 1961, when
the whole field was carefully gone through
over again and a programme worked out
closely knitting the various aims of the Survey
together.
This gradual unfolding of the aims of the
Survey prevented my colleagues from adopting
as many villages as they had originally intended to. But I believe that what may have been
lost in quantity has been more than made up

NEW DELHI

May, 24, 1963

v
for in quality. This is, perhaps, for the first
time that such a Survey has been conducted in
any country, and that purely as a labour of
love. It .has succeeded in attaining what it set
out to achieve; to' construct a map of village
India's social structure. One hopes that the
volumes of this Survey will help to retain for
the Indian Census its title to 'the most fruitful
single source of information about the coun·
try'. Apart from other features, it will perhaps be cenceded that the Survey has set up
a new Census standard in pic.torial and graphic documentation. The schedules finally
adopted for this monograph have been printed
in an appendix.

I

[N 'Simla Present and Past' there is a reference about the route to Seoui
"the route lies along Mashobra, Naldehra and Basantpur on a uood road
::0
which runs at times through "thick forest with lovely scenery."

I have seldom seen about 35 miles of such lovely scenery about the
variety of colour in Spring right up to Seoni and Tatapani. The route about
which Sir Edward mentioned was the road surveyed by Col. Phillimore. It
was a beautiful zig zag road to Shakrori which went. up to Seoni and Tataparli from 7,000 to 1,500 feet. We also pass Naldehra that fascinating green
which had captivated Lord Curzon so much that he named one of his
daughters Alexandra Naldehra Curzon (Lady Alexandra Metcalfe).
Going down to Shakrori, Seoni or Tatapani in Spring is enchanting.
Near Simla it starts with the apple blossoms, apricot blossoms, peach blossoms, near blossoms, coral flower and ends with kachnar and anarkali. 'The
seft red of pomegranate blossoms'.
Taking a decade and more, a change worth mentioning is the addition of a wonderful new road from Naldehra to Tatapani now passes
near Shakrori. This is one of the major roads in Himachal Pradesh after
1946. There has been a steady decline in the death rate. Chances of new
born living a full span of life have considerably increased. The major development of a new road has brought to their lives a slight change in fashion
and culture. Elders used to look at Chaba there were few electric bulbs and
had never hoped that they would ever see their homes electrified during
their life time. This has now become a reality.
Lying on the banks of the Sutlej, Shakrori has always been a prosperous
village. In the past children were a little shy of the school~ patients shy of
the doctor and the people were used to working less on the roads or
,,,orking outside their homes. Now more children go to the schools, even
girls go to schools, whenever there is illness, they seek the aid of the doctor.
There is a steady decline in death rate and the expectancy of life has increased. Villagers are slightly less dogmatic about their attitude towards
Harijans. Activities of the Community Development and Panchayat have
started creeping into their lives. The bethu system has been abolished. The
vilfagers are fairly well informed about offices located in Seoni and in Simla
or about what to get from where because Seoni has always been a place
of importance in their lives. They are still shy of taking to horticulture. A
number of old houses have been replaced by new ones and many altered
sub&tantially, though a number of owners are still using grass-roofs. This is
not so much because they cannot afford C.G.I. sheets but because C.G.I.
sheets are not available. Timber seems to be one of their problems.

humour, these days passed off happily. The village Lambardar Kundan Lal,
Naib Sarpanch Ram La!, Parma Nand, Loomcha, Behari and Sarpanch of
Shakra, all gave us much of little bits of information which helped us to

1 The Village
Ace 0 R DIN G to the village misal hakiyat, Shakrori a village in Seoni Sub·
Tehsil of Mahasu District derives its name from Shakri (small stones or
pebbles). At one time the village remained a bed of the river Sutler, before
it changed its course. As a result, the land now forming Shakrori was covered
all over with pebbles which are in abundance there even today. This pretty
village situated on the banks of the Sutlej is sprawled by neat little houses.
A portion of Seoni sub-tehsil is hilly but if we were to take the villages
in the lower valley of the tehsil, they are almost plain. The village is situated
at a height of 1,700 ft. above sea level and lies near Seoni on the Simla-SeoniTatapani road. The average monthly rainfall and the mean monthly tetpperature recorded at Chaba Electric Power House during the last 10 years, is
given in table I.
Tn winter there may seldom be a snowfall though it gets pretty cold with
mercury going down to 41°P. Spring and autumn are very pleasant but
summer months are hot and the temperature goes up to more than 101°F.
Most of the year the village remains green and the fields have a bountiful
of crops.
Shakrori was selected for our survey because it was one of those medium
sized villages with a population of about 342, having an agricultural bias.
One third of the area is irrigated by a kuhl. During the last 15 years the
changes that have occurred here are in the form of the merger of the State
to form a part of Himachal Pradesh; a well-settled motor-road; ele~tricity;
marketing facilities being near Simla; opportunities to work on the road or
to take up jobs in Simla. The village has changed in some ways but has
remained static in others. An attempt is made here to study these aspects of
social and economic life of the villagers.
Legend about the Village
Elders have narrated that once there lived an old man about two furlongs
away from Shakrori, close to the Sutlej. Tn due course the old man's son
married a beautiful girl. After a few months the lady fell ill. She recovered
after a long treatment and one evening when she came out to wash her 'thalu
(bronze plate) after serving the meals to her father-in-law. she heard a voice
coming from the facing dhank (cliff) calling parhu, parhu, parhu ('am falling).
This phenomenon continued for over six months. Since only the daughter-inlaw heard this, she was awed by the mysterious call and her health started
waning. The old man showed signs of anxiety and finally the whole phenomenon was narrated to him.
Next evening the old man accompanied his daughter-in-law when she
went out to wash the utensils. He suggested to her that the next evening
she might ask the voice to f~ll if it so wislwd,

2

THE VILLAGE

She acted accordingly and suddenly the
hillock rolled forward and swallowed the entire village including her house. Sounds of
musical instruments like dhol and nagara continued to be heard from the debris for about
a week.
The hill slide blocked the flow of Sutlej as
a result of which a reservoir was formed engulfing Shakrori and the neighbouring villages.
In due course when the river forced its way
through the obstruction, the ceded land was
left covered with pebbles or shakri, which are
found in and around Shakrori even today.

Sections of the Village
The central area of the village, where the
main population now resides, is said to be 200
years old. In the beginning the Brahmans
used to live in Rajhera and Rajputs resided
in ParH Ber sections of Shakrori. Nearly 4
generations ago the Brahmans started building their houses in the heart of. the vil1ag~,
/since the residential accommodatIOn of Rajhera was not sufficient for their requirements
and also because their families became larger
and wanted more space to spread out.
A few years later, Rajputs also started
shifting from Parli Ber ~o~ s~ilar reaso~s. The
Lahar families are resIdmg 111 three dIfferent
sections viz., 3 households in Parli Ber, 6 in
Gaullu (one of the settlements on the north)
and the remaining 3 in the main abadi.
There are only 2 Chamar households. One
of them resides in the main abadi and the
other in Gaullu.

The households are divided iin five categories according to their settlement history.
1. More than five
11 Rajput families,
11 Brahman and 6
Lahar families.
2. Between 4 and 5
generation
4 Rajput and 1
Lohar.
3. Between 2 and 4
generations.
11 Rajput and 1
Lohar.
4t One generation.
3 Rajput, I Chamar
and 3 Brahman
families.
5. Present generation ... 1 Brahman, 1 Rajput, 2 Lahar and 1
Sood.
Drinking Water Sources and Kuhl
Drinking water is fetched from springs in
!he village. The first spring, one comes across,
IS on ~ay to the main abadi near the motor
road, from where the village path hegins. This
spring Panhetu-ka-pani serves the drinking
water needs of three or four households who
have recently constructed their' farm-cumdwellings in the nautor land. The water is
hard.
Bain-ke-pas-ka-pani is just a few feet below
the Primary School building near an old
baoli or bain which has now dried up. This
spring has, a cemented raised platform with a
small out-let pipe. The drinking water sources
are open to all without any restrictions of
caste or creed; generally people living on the
west of the Shivalaya get water from here while
those living in Parli Ber and on the eastern
side of the Shiva temple get their water from
J akhar-ka-pani.
RajJ:zere-ka-pani is considered to be the
sweete'st and coldest but it is so far away from

At the spring

THE VILLAGE

3

the main abadi and other sections that few
households get water from here. The three
springs in Gaullu known as Gaullu-ka-pani
serve the Lohars and a Chamar who live
close by.
There is also a kuhl in the village which is
primarily used to irrigate the paddy fields. But
some households who find it difficult to fetch
water from the springs generally meet their
drinking water needs from the kuhl.
The springs generally derive their names
either from the place where they are situated
or from some important landmark nearby. For
example, Rajhere-ka-pani is so known because
it is located in Rajhera and Bain-ke-pas-kapani derives its name from the dried up bain
(baoli) near which it is located. '
Communications

Roads-There is a well settled motor road
that links Simla with Seoni, Tatapani, Karsog
and goes on to Mandi. This is one of the major
developments after 1948, but it is after 1958
that the road has settled down so well. Buses,
trucks and jeeps ply throughout the day. A
road to Luri also passes through this village.
Then the village paths and pakdandis connect Shakro'ri with Matyogri, Bagain, Chaba
and Devi Dhar-the neighbouring villages.
lhoola-There is a jhoola over the river Sutlej very near to Chaba and the villagers use it
to go across the river to Shakra and other villages. It was built sometime in 1953. Sometimes they ferry across on an inflated buffalo

Kachnar
for Shakrori on his way from and to Seoni.
Very often the children, take post cards and
letters with them to the post office at Seoni,
while on their way to the Higher Secondary
School. They also collect the dak for their
parents from the Sub-Postmaster.

skin either from .chaba or from just below
the village. T arus generally charge two annas
per head for ferrying across. Some villagers
may pay even Re. 1 to Rs. 2 for going down

one mashak then a passenger lies across the

Post Office-The nearest post office is located at Chaba. The dak runner delivers the dak

There are quite a few trees in the village
which provide timber. The varieties of simbal,
tun, shisham, kharak and khair are found in
and around Shakrori. Last year, the villagers
sold a large number of khair trees to the
kattha makers and hence only a few of them
are now left growing here. The buel trees are
also available in the village. Their green leaves
serve as fodder while their branches supply
raw material for the ropes besides firewood.
Bamboos available near springs in the village
arc owned by eight households. Kachnar and
coral are also found which look pretty when

Residential Pattern
Grown of a cluster of dwellings, the houses
have come up in no regular way. There are
no proper lanes and these cluster of houses
are linked with each other through rough and
ill marked passages and very often one has
to cross some fields ?'r water channels to get
from one housE' to the other. Previously Rajhera and Berh on the eastern part of the village were a residential area. Rajputs and Brahmans lived in Rajhera and Berh respectively.

Hearsay goes that Brahmans came to Rajhera fr~m Rajooi and other villages in the
sub-tehsil. Before that the story goes tha.t two
Brahman-brothers had come from Sirmur and
settled in Rajooi. They quarrelled with each
other and one of them moved to Rajhera several generations ago. At present it is the 5th
or 6th generation of the Brahman families
which are ~til1 residing in this village. Because of their distant relationship and kin,
ship tlle Brahmans do not inter-marry in the
village. As the family started growing, they
shifted to the dogris or farm houses which
had been built up earlier. These dogris were
later repaired and by making some additions
and alterations in many of them, they were
converted into permanent residences.
Rajputs also give the same reason for their
shifting from Berh to' Shakrori. Some of them
came here, more tban 3 generations ago, from
the adjoining villages.

Lohars of GauIlu came from Karsog tehsil
a few generations ago. Some nautor lands .have
been given since 1948 and many of them are
broadly tucked upon the river beds. It is
gathered that at one time the land at Gau11u
was left unattended Ul the name of the devta.
Only three generations ago, it was given to a
Lohar from Suket as a gift, because he had
won favour of the ruler. Another version of
this is quite plausible. A Harijan was brought
from Suket to serve as a menial because the
local Harijans could not cope witi] the work.
The Lahar family of Gaullu was the richest
at one time. There ruay be a number of
reasons for this. The family may be well accomplished~ there may be tremendous demand
of their labour; the expenses of the family
may be to the bare minimulU and lastly, it
may be because of economic farming. Besides the Lohars, a Chamar f~mi1y also lives
in Gaullu.

established with these villages.
Adjoining Villages and Places of Interest

The most interesting place nearby is Cha~a
because' it has one of the oldest Hydro-electnc
Power Houses in India. This was opened in
the year 1913. An extract from 'Simla Past
and Present' (page 116) by Sir Edward Buck
is reproduced here:Scarcely any visitor of Simla has an idea
as to how the station procures her
electric light, so a few words on the
Hydro-Electric Scheme will not be
out of place. About fifteen years ago
Captain, now Colonel B. C. Batlye,
R.E .• who will always be remembered as one of Simla's greatest benefactors, was placed on special. duty
to work out the technical detaIls of
a scheme proposed by Major General
Beresfrord Lovett, R.E. The necessary power was eventu.ally found by
damming up the NauU khud. s~ream
beyond Mashobra, and prov!dmg a
heavy fall near the Sutlcj river. The
cost of scheme was approximately
thirteen lakhs-'of rupees, At one time
about 2,000 men were engaged on
the works, the labour being mainly
supplied by gangs of Pathans from
the frontier, men from Busha?r
State, and Baltis from the lake dIStrict. The ordinary hillman of the
Simla district was toO busy with his
fields to work in anything but a fitful manner. Coal strikes and booms
in the steel trade in England caused
some unavoidable delays, and considerably inconvenienced the wor~
at times.

population has bad the cunoslty to

visit. the head works at Chaba which
lie near the Sutiej about twenty miles

from Simla. The road thereto is by no
means uninteresting as it leads in the
first place to Mashobra, thence to
Nalderq., the site of the golf Jinks, and
then descends about ten miles .down
a 'picturesque slope which is quite
well wooded with the ordi.nary Himalayan scrub and brushwood. The
Nauti river has been trained for two
and a half miles in a concrete flume
along the open hill side. through
tunnels, one of which is 400 yards
in length, and at one point across a
nallah, until it finally runs into a
large reservoir about 450 feet long
by 120 feet wide and 12 feet in
depth.
This reservoir which cost a lakh and a
half to construct holds nearly seven
million gallons of water and is situated on the top of a spur over looking
both the Nauti khudQund the Sutlej
river, and viewed froUl the opposite
side of the river looks a very remarkable lake indeed. When the water
is not required it escapes down the
side of a rock and flows into the
Sutlej below forming a beautiful
waterfall. The wate·r for the purpm.e
of driving the turbines is carried in
a vertical fall in five great iron pipes
540 feet down the mountain side to
the power house, where can be seen
the wonderful electrical plant which
was all imported from England, tra~sported from Simla over the mountam
road, and erected in this far away
valley. The power house is most picturesquely situated, only a few feet
above the Sutlej, and .t~e eJ?-ginee~s
incharge are most obhgmg 1ll thelf
efforts to explain generators, voltage,
transformers kilowatts and other
mysteries to the intelligent visitors:
Whatever may be said of the cost of
the scheme: the engineering feat
which has been accomplished is a
very remarkable on~, f<?r the trans·
portation of the macllmery was a
difficult task, and Captain Batlye, assisted bv Khan Bahadur Abdui
Ahad Executive Engineer and Mr.
F. r. Milne, Electrical Engineer ~nd
his staff, performed a wonderful pIece

Zedwin-This village also lies across the

ihis weaver who is known ior his humility,

strikes as one approaches the springs. A ba~h
in these springs is said to cure many skIll
diseases. A forest rest house and a Tourist inn
are also located here. Large number of villagers
gather here for a bath on the occasion of
Lohri-a seasonal festival, held on the Ist of
Magh every year.

2 The'People
THE main communities are Brahmans, Rajputs and Lohars. The original
settlers are said to be the Brahmans. Rajputs came next. They brought some
persons belonging to Scheduled Castes from outside, for menial services
with them. The castewise village composition is given in table V.
The Sood family belonging to the Va ish caste is non-agriculturist and
runs a shop here. Every household possesses land which they cultivate.
The Scheduled Caste families have small holdings. Inspite of the difference
in social status people belonging to different castes live in harmony. although
SCheduled Castes do still observe some of the inhibitions imposed upon
them since times gone by. Old customs die hard.

PopUlation
The village population comprises of 342 persons 73 percent of which
are below 34 years of age-(58 boys and 81 girls in the age group of 0-14,
and 54 each in 15-34). In the age group of 35-59 there are 42 men and
34 women which form 21 per cent of the total popUlation. There are only
9 men and 8 women above sixty, constituting 6 per cent of the village population, besides two elderly women who could not state their age. The percentages of the popUlation when compared with those in the District and
the Pradesh reveal that the percentage of children in the age group of 0-14
in the village is the largest and that of the aged people is tne lowest, as compared with the District and Pradesh figures. The percentage popUlation in
the age group of 15-34 and 35-59 in the village almost conforms to the
District and Pradesh averages.
I
~ Larger number of children in the age group of 0-14 gives an indication towards more than average increase in the village. But there is
another factor for the increase of population, that is, there should be more
of males than the females so that they bring more women in the village than
what they marry away since marriages are generally held outside the village. There are more girls than the boys in this age group. Information collected snows that for every 58 girls married away only 42 would be brought
to Shakrori by marriage. As compared to this the District and the Pradesh
have more boys than girls.
Inter Caste Relationship
Rajputs, Brahmans, Kumliars and Soods mix freely and inter-dine witli
each other. There was a stage when the Brahmans used to observe chauka
strfctly by changing their clothes and sitting for a meal in the kitchen and
would only eat particular type of meal, whenever, they were asked out. This
practice is not strictly followed now. They, however, do not at all intermarry and the frame of the caste system is observed.
*Sir Danziel Ibbetson has classified the Cultivating Class of Rajputs, in
the Punjab Hill States as Kanets.

12

THE PEOPLE

The Scheduled Castes are also invited by
these swarns on occasions of marriages and
other festivals or rejoicings but they are served
meals separately and invariably last of all.
Due to caste taboos which are prevalent in the
village from olden times all the Scheduled
Castes will have odd simple single storeyed
houses. They generally do not use a palki
(palanquin) for marriages. Musical instru-

ments like nagara, dholak or shahnai are also
seldom used. A Lohar widow, however, possesses several items of furniture which even the
swarns do not possess and recently when her
daughter was married. she was sent off in a
palanquin and the nagara, local shahnai and
other local musical instruments formed the
orchestra. Nobody in the village seemed to
mind.

THE PEOPLE

Table V indicates that 65 per cent of the
households have four to six members and less
and that every fourth household in the village
consists of a family of three or less. This is
a pointer to the fact that there is a tendency
towards the splitting up of the complex households into simple ones. This is, because when
younger members grow up they start earning,
from n<;m-agricultural sources like service in
Government or private offices or as labourers
on the road. They develop a sort of individuality and thus wanes the authority of the
head of the household who was at one time,
all in aU, as -he was the proprietor of the
household land-the only source of income to
the family. Table VI also reveals the same
fact that the families in the village are splitting up. There are only 14 out of 60 households in the village which have joint families.
as against 36 households having simple and
single member families.

Houses
Generally the houses are kacha, having an
attractive mud plaster and built on a rectangular plan using mud and stone. More than
half of the houses are two storeyed, and the
rest are single storeyed. There are 12 houses
of the Scheduled Castes, most of which have
grass 'roofs. The houses of the swarns have
roofs of slates, iron sheets, bamboos and
kacha stones. These houses have started
having glass windows and ventilators. The
obras or the cattle sheds have straw roofs.
During the last decade slates and iron sheets
have come up for more use. There is dearth
of timber in the village. The timber and slates have to be brought from far off places at
market rates when one has to build a good
house. Timber is brought from Badmahan (10
miles) and slates from Kufri (20 miles)
where there are slate quarries or from Pangna
in Karsog Tehsil (22 miles). The farmers prepare windows and doors mostly from tuni and
kikar trees growing' in their own lands. The
rafters for the roofs sometimes have to be of
bamboos.

This shows that the villagers have ample accommodation. The mud plaster is generally
bileous yellow, or light-brown, the colour of
the local matti.
The ground floor portion of the house can
be divided into two parts, viz., biyoon and
akhal.
Biyooll~These are the rooms on the back
and are used for sleeping during hot summer
months; or for use by the women during their
unclean days; or else, for keeping foodgrains
and other things. In some houses, one of the
rooms in the biyooll is also used as an obra
and in this cattle are kept. Child I)irth also
takes place in biyoon.
A khal-This is a big room at the entrance
and is used by women for stitching, se~ing
and spinning purposes.

Ola-Portion of wall above the plinth.
Dwar-Door.
Bee or Akhli-Veranduh, in the lower
storey. In this the staircase is located. It also provides entrance to the
rooms in the lower storey.
Biyoon-Rooms in the lower storey.
Pauri-Staircase made of stone.
Paura-Verandah above the Bee or
Akhli.
Dwari-Window.
Kothri-Rooms in the upper storey.
MerlI-Floor.
Chhallall (Attic)-Spare room between
roof and ceiling.
Chhaper-Roof.
Sharota-A wooden log with notches cut
in it providing steps, used as a lad
der.
Before, constructing a house the owner consults a priest about the site and the auspicious
day when the construction is to be started.
Havan is performed in the beginning and
then foundations are dug to a depth of 3 to 4
fect. These are filled -with rubbles and stones
but no mortar is used. The plinth is raised
about two feet above the ground.

Paura and kothris . . arc the rooms above
aHal and biyoon. While one of the kothris is
used as a rasoi or kitchen, rest are used as
bed rooms. Entrance to the upper storey is
through aklzal where a pauri (staircase) connects it to the paura.

Then the process for erecting walls is taken
in hand. The wall in the local dialect is called
bhit or matkanda.

The villagers have local names for different
parts of the houseThani (Courtyard)-Portion of land facing the front of the house.

Construction
No big stones or bricks are used while erecting the walls. A wooden frame 1t ft. wide
is prepared and laid on the spot where the wall

18

is to be erected. A mixture of moist sand,
small stones and clay is poured into the frame
to the top level. The filling is beaten W.: . ll
wooden beaters by the members of the family
and relatives till it becomes hard and compressed. This is left to dry for a day or so
and in the meantime the other portion is prepared. The wooden frame is again fixed on
the dry portion of the wall and filled with the
mixture referred to above and beaten with the
wooden beaters. The walls of the house or
the rooms are raised to the roof level. Doors,
windows and ventilators are marked, as the
construction progresses, and a portion of the
bhit is scouped out after the filling is beaten
and levelled.
Sometimes, bamboo sticks,
split horizontally, are also inserted and these
serve against possible burglaries.
Thus the construction of the bhit is akin to
the construction of the concrete wall, where
the latter is poured in the frame, with the only
difference, that the concrete is allowed to set
by itself while the moist mixture of sand, pebbles and clay here is made to set by manual
labour. The rafters are laid lengthwise and
tin, slate or thatched roofs in two to four
slopes are constructed. In case a second
storey is required flat wooden planks are used
which serve as the floor of the second storey.
No pucca cemented roofs are laid. Just
about 2 to 3 feet below the roof a sort of ceiling locally called chhanan is constructed. This
provides ample space for storing household
things. The house is plastered with a mixture
of clay and cow-dung from inside and outside
and in some cases mud wash or whitewash
is done by local mitti known as makol. The
carpenters and masons are paid daily wages
of Rs. 2 to 3 plus meals and tea.
An auspicious day is fixed fat griha-pravesh, when lzavan is performed and navgrehas
(Nine Planets are worshipped. This ceremony is known as grehasni and a feast is given
to friends and relatives. It is after grehasni
that the family moves into the new house.
Dogris-Some villagers, whose lands are far
off from main abadi. have built farm houses
or dogris near their fields. They live there
during the working season as they find it convenient to work more and also keep a watchful eye on their growing crops. Dogris are
alike in structure, to the dwelling houses in
the main abadi. except that the accommodation in the former is rather small. In 3 or 4
cases, the purpose behind constructing the
dogris was the same as mentioned above, but

THE PEOPLE
they have now been converted into' regular
dwelling houses. This was necessitated
because of the increase in size of some families
Who found it difficult to live in their old house
together. They have their meals from the common produce of crops though their kitchens
in the dogris. where they now live regularly,
are split up.
Khatr Katthia House-Recently a Lahar
has constructed a house of bamboo strips and
date palm leaves. It has a thatched roof that
cost the Lahar only his labour. He managed to
get the material free. This type of house is
generally used by kattha con~actors and the
Lohar picked up the construction technique
from them.

A

Thatched hOllse

Obra-It is a cattle shed built on a rectangular plan. The entrance to' the obra is
through a small door in the front. No other
ventilator is provided to this structure except
that some air passes in from the small openings near the thatched roofing. The roofs are
thatched with grass brought from Moongna
(neighbouring village-5 miles). Sometimes
these are also thatched with wheat straw. The
roofing is generally replaced every 3rd or 4th
year. The wall construction process 'of the
obra is the same as used for regular dwelling
houses. Khunte are dug on the floor for tying
cattle.

Furniture
The homes of the villagers are simply furnished and they possess small items of furniture. Mostly hinnas and manjris of parai
(rice straw) are spread over as mats.
Some
possess klwrchas made of goat hair. Well-todo zamindars possess one to two charpoys,
one to two tables and some chairs for guests.

Dress

The male dress consists ofUPPER GARMENTS

Ambll Topi--Made of cotton cloth it
covers only the top portion of the
head. It may be called a skull cap.
Kishti Topi-May be woollen or cotton.
Bushahri Topi-A head dress of Bushahr.
People purchase it either from
Simla or from Rampur Bushahr.
Baaldar T opi-A sort of fur cap and
resembles the kishti !opi.
Topa-A woollen ~ap which may be
opened to cover the/neck also. in addition to the head and ears. Only
the face is left uncovered. In Simla
it is known as monkey cap.
Saafa-Some older people also wear
saafa or muslin turbany In State
times the wearing of saafa was very
common when the villagers had to
go to darbar. Now the use of saafa
is disappe,aring fast. Young men do
not Pllt on any head dress.
Kameej-A shirt with collars and sleeves
with cuffs. There are generally two
pockets. one in the front and the
other on the right side of the waist.
Kur!a-A collar-less and cuff-less shirt
slightly longer than the ordinary
Kameej.
People sometimes use

1

1

silver buttons instead of the ordinary
ones with it. The shirts are stitched
by a local tailor and the cloth may
be hand woven or coarse mill made
one.
Sadri-It is a waist coat and may be of
cotton or woollen cloth depending
on the season. It is still very common with the older people though
some young mel~ also wear it during
the winter months.
Band gale ka Coat and ChllOta CoatThese coats are either close necked
or the open necked type with 3 buttons. Generally they are made of
patti or some rough tweeds. Sometimes. the villagers also purchase
ready made coats from the second
hand garments-shop at Simla or at
Seoni where a stall is opened during
some important fairs like Baisakhi
or Dussehra.
LOWER GARMENTS

Pajamas, . Rebdar Pajama,
Churidar
Pajama and Khllli mohri-ka-pajama
-The pajamas are made of cotton
cloth. In winter some villagers wear
pajamas of patti. The woollen paja-mas are rather baggy upto their
knees while the lower portion covering the legs is a little narrow. The
difference between the rebdar pajama and the churidar pajama is that
the latter is tighter at the legs.
The cotton pajamas worn may be either
white or coloured ones. Striped
pajamas are also worn.
Knickers-The school boys sometimes
wear Khaki knickers (shorts) as a
part of their uniform.

FOOT WEAR

Men-Derby Shoe and Full Slipper-Both are made in the village' by local
artisan and cost from Rs. 6 to 15 depending on the size of the shoe and
the material used.
Chamba Chappals, Fleets and other
ready made shoes are also bought
from shops at Simla and Seoni.
Women-Pump Shoe and Do phite ki
gurgabi-Both are prepared by the
village shoe maker. Ladies shoes are
purch'ased from the shops at Simla
and SeonL
There is a variety of shoes worn, depending
on what is available at "Seoni and Simla and
the money they want to spend. The women
do not wear shoes while going to the fields or
for roaming about in the village. They move
about bare footed.

Ornaments
Silver ornaments were very common a generation ago. The ornaments worn, used to be
heavy. Now leaving aside tilli for nose and
clwk for head which are used by married
women as suhaag ornaments, no other golden
or silver ornaments are worn. Older women,
however, still adorn themselves with silver on
occasions like marriages.

Some ornaments
The ornaments worn by the village ladies
on festive occasions areORNAMENTS FOR THE

HEAD

Chak-A hemispherical hollow ornament
made of silver with designs carved

THE PEOPLE

all around it. It is worn by all married ladies, young or old, at the top
of the head, and weighs between 80
to 100 grams.
Clips-These are either made of silver or
gold and are wo.t;n on both sides of
the head for keeping the hair in
place.
ORNAMENTS FOR THE EARS

Dandis-Made of silver each weighs
about 5 to 6 grams. Older women
wear them. Four to six dandis are
worn on each ear for which separate
holes are pierced already.
Bragars-A pair weighs about 15 to 20
grams, and is fringed with pearls. It
is made of silver and is worn on special occasions by old and young
ladies alike.
Kanta-A bell shaped piece, fringed
with metal suspended from the ear,
by a hook. These are made of gold
and are used by young ladies.
Tops-A star shaped jewelled earring
made of gold or silver.
ORNAMENTS FQR THE NOSE

Laung-A small golden stud set with a
pearl or turquoise, weighs about 6
grams.
Tilli~Miniature laung weighing 2 grams
is used by almost aU married ladies
regularly.'
Baltl~A small golden pendant attacheti
to the nose. worn at the time of marriages or while attending fairs and
festivals.
Besar_:_A big pendant of gold or silver
attached to the nose and supported
by a chain hooked in thehead.
ORNAMENTS FOR THE NECK

K anglloo-A bracelet· of silver weighs

ORNAMENTS FOR THE ANKLES

Panjeb-A common name for different
type of ornaments made of silver
chains tied round the ankles. The
chains clink together when one
walks.
Tora-A type of panjeb with a clasp.
Silver buttons are also used by some men
and women for their shirts.
The ornaments of gold and silver are pre·
pared by a goldsmith of Seoni. Women do
not put on many ornaments. The silver ornaments which are rather heavy, used to be put
on by the older ladies. They also do not wear
them except on some festive occasions like
marriages.
Unmarried girls only wear bangles while
the married women put on only a laung or a
filii in the nose.
Dandi was very common among the older
generation in the past and they used to wear
as many as 4 or 5 dan dis according to the
number of holes pierced in the ears. Kanta,
Bragar or tops are also worn by them.
Cheap immitation ornaments such as a
Kanthi of brass is very commonly used by the
women.

M stands for married, S for separate amI W for widow in Col. 4.
Special dish of kheru:is prepared as a substitute of dal, Tassi, turmeric, ahee and salt heated together whioh make kheru.

Festive Occasions-On ~ festive occasions
food generally includes the use of ghee and
shakkar. The following dishes and items of
food are also preparedRotku-Fried sweetened chapa ties
of
wheat flour.
/'
Bhalla-Prepared of pithi (minced urad).
Sanse-Prepared ' on a leaf of barh
<bodhi)-tree. which serves as a
form. On the leaf, dough of very
thin solution is poured, and then it
is steam-cooked by putting it in a
metal utensil tambia. When this
chapati like substance is cooked. it
is removed from the leaf and then
fried in deep fat in a karahi.
Babru-Sweetened thin chapa ties are prepared on a tawa in a shallow fat.

The flour used is either of wheat -or
of batlzu.
A.~'kaloo-These are prepared of wheat
(a
flour and are baked in a form
stone piece having small holes).

Chillies

2~

THE PEOPLE

Patunda-Prepared with wheat flour thinned with water. It is then baked on
a tawa into thin chapaties; taken with
ghee shakkar.
Sour Pulses-(Khatti daD and sweet pulses (mithi daD prepared by adding
anardana and shakkar respectively
instead of salt.
Berhi Roft-Berhi roti prepared of kolira
flour with a filling of kachalu is eaten
during the manuring season called
gabrain (December-January).
Barhi-Prepared by grating thor (cactus) locally called sarua which is
mixed with pithi (minced urad). into
a dough of thick consistency. Small
balls prepared from this dough are
dried in the sun.
Sigra-Wheat flour is turned into dough
and is filled with till (Sesamum). Its
cakes are then steam cooked in a
tambia and eaten with ghee shakkar.

Khatta Shikar-Meat prepared by adding a lot of anardana from wild
pomegranate.
The people well off and infants. all take
milk while others take it in the form of lassi
or khairu. Butter and ghee are also common
in domestic use. Gur is consumed more in
winter. Villagers like to make anardana out
of dam (wild pomegranates) and also make
some pickles of mango. lemon and galgal.
Use of chillies is also very common-chillies
of this area are very famous all over Himachal.
Now they arc also getting used to vegetables
and grow lady-finger, tomatoes, bitter gourd.
brinjal, carrot, radish. turnip. onion, garlic and
linda. Chapaties of maize flour are greatly
relished with sag of mustard leaves and lassi.
People also collect a lot of mangoes, jujufus
and ga/gal from wherever, they can lay their
hands on. Mangoes are found in abundance
in and around Shakrori and during the season,
people generally take chapaties with raw
mangoes or else, they use them to sour preparations which are very much relished.

3 Birth, Marriage and Death Customs
Birth

N 0 prenatal ceremony seems to be performed and the expectant mother
leads. a n~rmal life except that she does not exert herself much and is not
permItted to carry loads. T~is. is a precaution against miscarriage or abortion
and not because of any rehgIous or customary belief.
The birth of a male child, is heralded with great joy accompanied by
many religious ceremonies depending upon the social and economic status
of the parents of the new born. The customary belitfs and practices observed in the village on this occasion are given in the following lines:
The delivery takes place in biyooll-a room in the ground floor and is
helped by mother-in-law assisted by some other elderly woman in the neighbourhood. At the birth of a male child the materna] uncle presents a silver
d/'ubba while others give bouquets of turf to the parents. This is considered
equivalent to conveying congratulation. Time of birth is noted for preparing kundli (horoscope) of the child later. SUlak (unclean period) is observed
for ten days in the family where birth takes place and during this period
ladies daily sing behai in praise of the goddess of birth. On the 11th day
shuddhi (purification) ceremony is performed with havan and puja. On this
day, the house is cleaned and plastered. The mother is given a bath and
gangaja/ and pancll-gavya are sprinkled to purify her and then she puts on
new c1othes. The child's nam karan sanskar is also performed
and all the nine planets (grehas) are worshipped, in which the purohit
chants Vedic hymns. Offerings of foodgrains known as navgreh grant!zis are
also made for the quietude of the grehas. Out of these, three granthis offered
to Shani, Rahu and Ketu are given to a panda and the rest go to the
purohit who performs the ceremony. Paddy, wheat, a few naya paisa and
patundas offered to bellai (also known as Bhatiarka Devi) are
given to the dai. She also gets· a suit of clothes, some foodgrains, ghee and
sugar as a gift- for services rendered. Some people also perform Sat Narain's
Katlla on this day. Gantriala is also celebrated and a feast is given to the
relatives and bradri. On this occasion the maternal uncle and other near relatives of the child bring Kangnus (bracelets) of silver for the child and
Clothes for the mother.. The maternal uncle also presents one seer of gJzee,
some sugar or shakkar and about 4 seers of rice to the pafents.
In the case of a baby girl these ceremonies are not observed on such an
elaborate scale! as in the case of a boy. Purification ceremony of sprinkling
of panchgavya, havan and quietude offerings to the navgrehas are, however,
performed in case of a girl also. Information about her birth is given to the
maternal unc1e only and no feast is given to the bradr; (kins folk). Maternal
uncle of the child usually presents gifts of ghee, rice, sugar or shakkar to
the parents of the child even in the case of a girl.

28

BIRTH, MARRIAGE AND DEATH CUSTOMS

Chhatto
Chhatto ceremony is performed in case of
both boys and girls when they are six months
old. The purohit chants Vedic hymns, danpunya is performed and the child is given
kheer. This cerel!1ony is also known as ann
prashan, tasting of food by the child for the
first time. In some cases when gantriala cere!TIony (a feast given to' friends and relatives
on the 11th day of tl}.e birth of a male child)
could not be performed earlier because of unfavourable grehas, it is then performed at the
time of chhatto ceremony and a feast is given
to the bradri and relatives. The maternal uncle
gives rice and pulses (about one maund), ghee
and spices on this occasion. He also presents
a suit of clothes to the mother. and silver
kangnu to the child.
Mnndan and Karan Vedh
When a boy attains an age of 3, 5, 7 or 9
years his mundan and Karan vedh sanskars
are performed. Generally the ceremony is performed in the temple of their goddess Bhawani.
The pandits find the auspicious moments for
karan vedh after consulting the jantri and also
the child's kundli.
First of all, oil is poured on the head of" the
boy after which it is shaved. leaving some hair
on the top. The hair left are called shikha or
choti. Choti is a sign of purity and is believed
to remin!i the boy of his duties towards his religion. This ceremony is called mundan. In
case a child dies before his ceremony it is immersed in the river. It can be cremated only
after the mundan ceremony is performed. Af-

Doli
ter this the ears of the boy are pierced with a
golden needle. This ceremony is called karan
vedh. It is believed that karan vedh immunises
against many diseases.
During these ceremonies the ladies sing
mangal (auspicious) songs. A grand feast
(dham) is given to the entire village and
relatives. In case of the girls, the ceremony is
restricted to piercing of the ears. No other
functions are performed nor is any feast given.
Yagyo Pavit
Yagyo pavi! ceremony is contIlled to 15
families of Brahmans. On this occasion toran
mandua is fixed. Mustard oil and batna consisting of nihani, wheat flour, turmet:ic and
chamukri is applied to the boy two or three
times. Purohit then asks the boy to beg from
his relatives and whatever he gets in alms, he
gives it to the purohit in lieu of which the
latter gives guru mantra and re1igious instructions alongwith a yagyo pavit (sacred
thread) to wear. A dham is also given to
friends and relatives. This ceremony is performed in case of male children only and between the age of 13 to 17 years. The Rajputs
perform it at the time of their marriage.
Marriage
Table IX shows that there are 155 married
persons (72 men and 83 women) in the village
and that 11 men have more than one wife.
Another fact revealed by this table is that
marriage in the village is universal. All the
women who are more than 25 are married
and in the age-group of 15-24 there are 0!11y
three unmarried girls. There are onl~ 5 men
above 24 who are yet to marry. There is no
widow or widower o~ young age, because
widow marriage is prevalent here. Middle
aged persons and those with children, however, do not get remarried after the death of
their partners. There are no broken homes ex-

Marriage Customs-The different ways of
entering matrimony are marriage by negotiation, marriage by reet and gharjawai. But the
marriage by negotiations between the parents
of the prospective bride and groom is most
common in the village. The latter are not
consulted in such matters. The negotiations
are carried out by an elder or a purohit who
settles all the marriage ter,ms. Generally marriages do not take place in the same gotra excepting in stray cases. The main criterion in
selecting a match for the son or daughter is
that there should be no near or distant known
blood relationship between the couple.
Previously there used to be early marriages
when the girl would be married before she
attained- the age of twelve. Now this practice
is on the decline. Betrothal or mangni is the
first ceremony connected with the marJiage
and takes place at very tender age. When both
parties, the girl's father and boy's father have
consented to the match, the latter goes to the
girl's house accompanied by purohit and one
or two elderly persons. He also takes clothes
and ornaments for the girl. After performing
puja of Ganpati he distributes.-gur among the
villagers, and returns after having meals which
may consist of three varieties of dal-Ioonidal
(saIted) kllatidal (sour) and mithidal (sweet).
The boy's father also gives Rs. 1.25 to the
purohit of .the girl's side.
Table X shows that men are married when
they are more than fourteen years old. Thirty
four out of sixty nine were married in the age
groups of 14-20. Twenty one women were
married at the ages between ten and thirteen
whereas sixty two were married between
fourteen and twenty only one at the, age of

twenty two. (At the time of survey one woman
below fourteen and twelve between the ages
of 15-19 were found married).
Marriages in the village like marriages according to Hindu rites are associated with
many customs and ceremonies. First of all
purohits of both parties go to the jeotshi (astrologer) who prepares lagan patrika, after going
through the kundli of both the boy and the
girl. This patrika is prepared at least 15 days
before the marriage date is fixed. All' the ceremonies connected with the marriages are
performed on the dates mentioned ,in the lagan
patrika. This also gives the details and timings
when activities such as preparing of clothes,
pounding of pulses and rice and plastering of
the house are to be performed. '

Telalapan-At an auspicious moment members of the family, relatives and villagers come
up one by one and sprinkle oil with drub on
the head of the bride or bridegroom. Batna,
a paste prepared of barley flour flavoured with
orange rinds and powdered turmeric is also
applied to
soften their skin. The oil is
massaged for 5, 7 or 9 times according' to
what is written in the lagan patrika. These
ceremonies take place in the bride's house
and the bride-groom's house separately an_d
the timings may also be different.
Kangnoo Bandhna-A mauli is tied round
the wrists of both the boy and the girl and
many knots are given to it by relatives and
friends. A small iron ring, some grains of
mustard seed, betel nut (supari) and a· cowrie
or two are tied to this kangnoo. This ceremony also takes place separately in the homes
of the bride and the groom.
A Rajput bridegroom is given sacred thread
(Yagyopavit) at this time. Brahmans acquire
the right to wear this thread at an earlier age.
After bath the bridegroom w!!ars-the yagyopaviI and performs puja of all nakshatras,
naugrehas, Ganesh, Kshetrapal, Dwarpal, Indra, Brahma and Durga. He puts on clothes
given to him by his maternal uncle. These.
consist of a shirt, pyjamas or dhoti, sweater
or a waist coat, choga (a long coat), turban
(red coloured) murkalis (ornament for the
ears), and a sehra of tilIa which is. the most
important item of dress of the bndegroom.
The sehra resembles the crown or mukat
which is the head dress of Lord Krishna. Ra-

van is also performed and dan is given according to the financial capacity of the family
after which the varyatra begins. The bridegroom carries a sword and a cocoanut with
him and is accompanied by an escort called
ladsi. He goes in a palki (Palanquin) in case
he belongs to a swaran caste. Harijans either
ride a horse or go on foot.
When the bridegroom leaves for the bride's
house a thali (bronze plate) of ghee and a cow
or some ready cash representing its value are
given in alms. The bridegroom's party consists of bajantries (instrument players) who
play on shehnai, karnals; narsinghas, dhol and
naRaras and an attendant who carries suhag
palairi on his back. The suhag palairi is a
round box containing clothes, ornaments.
suhag pura, comb, mirror, dry fruits alld bindi
for the bride. Whoever carries these articles
also takes with him till-tallai (a pot containing
left over ubtan and oil with pice and a ring
dipped in it). After suhag patairi next in the
procession is the most important person the
bridegroom accompanied by a ladsi. Both of
them are carried in a palanquin. Last of all
are the relatives and friends of the bridegroom.
During the survey a Rajput girl was married
and the details here are based on the observations made on the spot by the investigator.

Toran-At bride's place toran (welcome
arches of bamboo sticks and decorated with
colours) are erected one near the vedi and the
other at the entrance to the house.

At the Vedi
Lagan-First of all 3 to 5 persons comprising of bridegroom's father and brothers go to
the bride's house, where toran is erected near
the mandap. They take suhag patairi and gur
shakkar. On one side of the loran the bridegroom's fa{;her and brothers sit while on the
other side the relatives of the bride. Here the
purohits from both sides chant mantras and
perform puja. The relatives of the bride are
introduced to those of the groom. The former
embrace the latter and pay money to them
at that time. After this preliminary ceremony
which is known as 11lilni and at the auspicious
moment bridegroom enters the mandap and
sits on the western side with his face towards
east while the parents of the bride sit facing
north. The priest then performs the shanti
pujan invoking the blessings of all the planets
for a successful marriage.

Now the puja is performed by the parents
of the bride through their purohit. First of all
they worship the bridegroom and then a fire
for the havan is lit. Women sing lagan amidst
chanting of mantras by the pandit.

The bride's maternal uncle escorts her to

clothes and other 'items which form dowry.
Relatives and friends also give presents to
the bride. All these items of dowry are kept
near the mandap pending further ceremony to
be performed. The bride sits on the right hand
side of the groom and the women sing songs
of kanya dan.

Next the bride's brothers perform havan
after which lavan ceremony begins. The lavan
or pheras consists of seven rounds (pheras,
round the holy fire). Three phera<; are ta ken
immediately after the fire is.lit. three after the
havan is performed and the last one at the end

33

of the ceremony. The bride leads in all the
first six pheras but follows the bridegroom on
the seventh. Women sing man gal songs
showering blessings on the couple wishing a
happy and prosperous married life. Married
women then apply tilak to the bride. The
bride now sits on the left hand siae of the
bridegroom as 'his lawfully wedded wife,
whose place. according to the Hindu beliefs
is always on the left of the groom. Hence forward she is Ardhangni-better-half. On this
occasion the bride wears new clothes consisting of a printed shirt. salwar and a dupatta.
Ornaments worn are only chak on the head.
nath in the nose and bragar in the ears. A
pair of kangnus is also sometimes worn by
the bride in the wrists. Leaving aside the
nath and bragar which are made of gold. all
other ornaments are made of silver. Kaliras
consistii1g of cowries. silver plates and cocoanuts threaded with mauli are also worn on
the wrists. The relatives and friends of the
family also give presents of clothes, utensils
and cash to the bride.
Maklava (Departure of the Barat}-At the
auspicious moment determined by the pandits,
the bride is seated in a covered palanquin
(dota). Her escorts- small children. <Laadar'
also accompany her in the dola. One man
from every household of the village also got's
with the bride to her in-laws place where they
are served with dal-bhat (Pulses and rice). The
bride. however. taKes her own meals (from her
father's place) with her and eats this for the
tirst day. At the time of departure of the bride, the
ladies bid farewell, so full of pathos-singing
of bidai. During the entire -marriage celebrations. this is the saddest moment ana tears
flood the eyes of almost all the ladies, young
girls and children present, besides the bride
who goes away in the dola, sobbing all the
way to her in-law's village. Men..also can not
restrain their feelings.

When the bride and bridegroom reach
their house. the groom dances in the presence
of all baratis; dan is given to the purohit; both
the bride and the bridegroom open the knots
of each others kangnas (the mauli tied round
their wrists at the time of tel ceremony); and
havan is performed near a pipal tree where
the bride and the bridegroom go together in
one palki. They also have seven rounds of the
tree, which is believed to ward off sterility in
a woman. When the brides relatives take
meals the women sing naughty songs (sithnian) which are full of puns.
At bride's house her parents serve a dham
(grand feast) to all the guests in the village
after the bride's departure to her husband's
house. Those who could not atfend are sent
their meals to their houses. This practice is
known as Priya.
Before marriage a girl is not considered impure during her menstruation period and
hence the marriage ceremony is performed
even if she is running the period. Her mother
and other relatives avoid entering the mandap
during their menstruation period, in case it
happens to coincide with the marriage date.

34

BIRTH, MARRIAGE AND DEATH CUSTOMS

In performing the Nandi mukh sharadh ceremony all the dead elders of the family are
worshipped five or six days prior to the
marriage. This confers upon the members of
the household the right to perform all ceremonies which are not otherwise permissible
during unclean periods. SImilarly the deothis
<earthen lamps) tied with the mandap cloth
with mauli are to sanctify everything done under the mandap.
Reet-Another form of marriage is by
'Reet'. If one whisks away another's wife the
new husband has to pay marriage expenses
and a penalty (between Rs. 100 to Rs. 500)
to the former husband. The divorce may acquire another wife after observing the 'Reet'.
Ghar lawai-A youngman from Suket was
made to stay in the house of an old lady who
had no children alive except a grand-daughter. He stayed there for a number of months
in the girl's house and proven helpful. He was
finally selected to marry the grand-daughter
and became the half owner of her property.
He now stays at her in-law's place. This is
commonly known as a Ghar J awai.
Death
Death raises an inevitable adjustment in
the family structure and creates many economic and social problems, large amounts are
spent-social taboos hinging on a very strict
code at times appear and many changes take
place. Some ceremonies are chiefly mentioned
here:
BEFORE DEATH

1. Godan (Dan of a cow).

2. Panch Ratna (consisting of gold, silver, airent, moonga and pearl) is put
in the mouth of the dying person.
3. Holy water (Gangajal) is poured on
the floor and then kusha leaves and
sesamum are spread thereon, on
which is laid the dying person.
4. The dying person then gives a dan of
satnaja (seven grains), salt. iron.
cotton, ghee, Iota (tumbler) Kansi-kithali (bronze plate), umbrella and
sword.
AFTER DEATH

1. The dead body is bathed with water
mixed with gangajal, clay from holy
places, and chandan. The body is

Funeral pyre

then wrapped in white cotton &heet
and decorated with flower garlands
it is placed in a box having silver
kalash, (if the dead one is old. otherwise he is laid on an arthi).
2. Plna dan-Pind is then made of barley
flour, ghee, honey and Sesamum and
offered on the following occasions of
the death rites.
i. When lifting the dead body for taking it to the crerpation ground.
ii. When the bier leaves the house.
iii. Mid-way between the house and the
cremation ground.
iv. When the bier is laid on the-pyre.

When the coffin is lifted and carried to the
cremation ground, musical instruments are
also played if the deceased is an elderly person.
On reaching the cremation ground near
Sutlej certain rites are observed.
1. Pind dan is offered.
2. Bier is placed on the pyre.
3. The pyre is lit; ghee and dried fruits
are poured.
4. Kapal Kriya is performed when the
body is nearly half burnt. In this the
head of the deceased is touched with
a bamboo staff by the person performing these rites. This is done under the belief that the soul of the
dead one leaves the body through
the skull. As a token this is done,

35

5. In the case of villagers the pyre is kept
lit for a part of the day only; the
, pyres of the important persons or
rulers are sometimes kept burning
for 3, 5 or even seven days.
6. The bones are then collected and sprinkled with the Holy water and Panch
Gavya. This rite is called Asthi Sanjay Sharadh.
7. The remains are brought home. For
ten days a pind dan is offered daily
and earthen lamp is kept burning in
the house. The sons shave their heads
and observe a strictness about food
and lie 011 the floor and do not 5have
for some days.

In the meanwhile the ashes are taken
Hardwar for immersion in the Ganges.

to

1. Kirya-The kirya rites by different
castes are observed on different dates,
the details of the rites are generally
common
amongst the different
castes.
Brahmans-After eleven days.
Kshatriyas-After thirteen days.
Vaisyas-After fifteen days.
Sudras-After fifteen days.
The following rites are performed during
kirya in case the death occurs on an inauspicious day.
i. Propitiation of the 10 Nakshatras
Grehas-Planets) ,
ii. A complete bedding with cot is
given in alms, shayya dan.
iii. Puja of Brahma, Vishnu and Shiva
iv. Havan.
2. Narayal) Bali: In this the pardon is
sought for,
i. Puja of Br.ahma. Vishnu, Shiva and
Ganpati.
ii. Havan.
iii. Dan of bed, shayya dan.

3. Brishoot Sarag: For peace to the departed soul.
i. Puja of Brahma, Vishnu. Sbiva.
ii. Havan.

iii. Dan of four cows and one bull.
4. On, I kadashi-Puja is performed for
purification of the deceased's soul
and in this a dan is given to vedwas
in the shape of expenses for the
whole year; dan of a bed, utensils
and a cow are also given.

S. Shodashi-It is also called 16 sharadha
and in this are given the dan of 16
plates (Thai us) 16 Iotas and dakshna.
6. Spindi-In this, pinds of rice are prepared in the names of three generation of the deceased and are given
in dan.
Every month on the date of death the Brahmans are given food grains for eleven months
and at the end of the year barkhi ceremony
is performed in which clothes bedding, ornaments, utensils and a cow or cash representing
its value are given in alms to the Brahmans.
Chaubarkhi is performed after four years of
the death and in this also charities of clothes
and utensils are given. After this the sharadh
of the deceased is performed every year in
Asauj in which the friends and relatives are
fed.
Sharadhs
In the month of Asauj every year the villagers perform sharadhs of all their ancestors
during the fortnight of krishan paksh. The
sam vat year is divided into lunar months of
28 days and then the lunar month is subdivided into two pakshas, the krishan paksh,
and the shukla paksh. Each of the 14 days
of krishan and Shukla paksh are called 'tithis'.
The sharadh of the ancestor is performed on
the tithi coinciding with one on which the
death took place. On this day the household
requests the services of a purohit ''''ho performs puja and makes pinds which they offer
to their ancestors. The household invites all
the villagers and relatives for a sharadh
(meal) consisting of rice. 100ili dal, mithi dal.
khati dal and tumbra-ki:,'iQbzi. All the persons
if they are Brahmans who come to have their
meals, are given dakshana in the shape of one
pice each. The meals are prepared on a large
scale. The person who cooks. is called' Boti'.
The expenses incurred by the household on
sueh an occasion are rather large coming to
about Rs. 100 (most of which is in the shape
of rice and pulses grown' by the household),

36

Because of this heavy expenditure on sharadh
the villagers perform one sharadh of a near
elder on a large scale and the rest of the sharadhs are performed on a small scale in which
only 2 or 3 purohits are invited for the ml"al.
The villagers believe that if they do not regularly perform sharadhs of their elders, they

BIRTH, MARRIAGE AND DEATH CUSTOMS

become prefs (evil spirits) and haunt the houses where they lived prior to their death.
People also serve meals to purohit,. and
relatives after one year of the death in their
household and this is called barkhi. A dan
of bed, clothes and utensils is given to the
purohits.
