Social & Cultural Life
THE villagers believe that their lives are governed by the dictates of various
gods. They are fatalists in real sense. The different gods and goddesses
worshipped in the village are Mahadev, Shiva, Kali Maata, Hanuman, Ram
Chandra, Vishnu, Bhagwati, Krishan, Narsingh, Lakshmi, Parvati and Durga.
They also worship pipal tree and lesser gods like Bir and Dano.
Shiva is worshipped by all but few go to the temple for prayers. Women
are not allowed to enter Shiva and Mahadev temples as it is believed Shiva
in his later life developed aversion to women. There is a taboo for ladies
to sit on a chair or to sleep on the cots.
Village Te_mpJes
A small pucca temple of Shiva facing west and built by Rana Ran
Bahadur Singh is located near the Pipal tree. Built on a circular plan it has
\:wo darner with a saffron flag flying and flower buds of satvarga and a baoli
outside it. There is Shivling in the temple; image of Ganesh is also phiced
in it.

Worship in this temple is performed occasionally durinng festivals or
marriages. Small offerings go To the pujari but bigger ones are credited to
the village heads who use it by rendering help to the needy persons at
marriages or deaths.
It is gathered that last year some villagers found a small image of Shiva
and his Nandi bull and a bell, while working on the Seoni-Luri road. All
these have been_lloused in the Shiva temple for the time being, pending a
gecision as to where these new findings are to be housed.
Another temple built on a rectangular plan in the outskirts of the village,
is of Mahadev. There, are seven metallic murtis of different sizes, believed
to be of Shivji's Avtar, enshrined in it. Mahadev is the deity of four villages,
(Shakrori, Nadukhar, Taror and Makarchha) and in every village, it has
its stay for two years. The deity is moved only during the nights and as
ladies are deprived of its darshans the departure and arrival of Mahadev
is announced beforehand and all the ladies keep away. Dhol and nagara
are played when puja of the devta is performed on every sankrant.
Dana Worship-At many places in and around Shakrori there arc sthans
of Dano or Sahasr Bahu where people offer rotis or atta-(flour) to the devta
on every t.sankrant. The original residence of the devta is at Seoni where
kharaon are offered.
It is said that once Jamdagani Rishi invited Dano for a yaina and borrow
ed kamdhenu cow from swargi (heaven) to entertain his guests. The cow used
to prepare all tasty dishes in no time. When the Dana saw this, he asked the
Rishi to give it to him. This was refused and as a result warfare started
between them in which the Dano was killed. People of Seoni sub-tehsil have

SOCIAL AND CUL'rURAL LIFE

great faith in the Dano. He owns land near
all temples. On the night of the sankrants,
oracles play at the stlwn while villagers ap.proach him for some remedy to their griefs
or sufferings.
Fairs and Festivals
The fairs and festivals celebrated are either
associated with religion or are seasonal. The
religious festivals are Shivratri, Holi, Laul-kaMela, Nirrijala-Ikadeshi, Mandhol-ka-Mela,
Rakhri, Digaili, lanam-Ashtmi, Dussehra and
Diwali.
Shivratri (Maha Shiv Ratri)-This is celebrated in Phalgun (February-March). Before
the great night, the villagers prepare rotlms
and also make garlands known as Gosain of
Camb, narcisus leaves and barley which they
hang outside their houses. Every household
makes an offering of one or two rotkus at the
temple in the evening. The daughters married outside are invited to join the celebration
and are served with rotkus.
Holi-This is a gay festival of fun and colours celebrated in Phalgun (February-March).
On this day Prahlad was saved from the clutches of his aunt Holika who wanted to burn
him to death in order to please her brother
Hiranakashyap. People gather in the morning
and start the day by throwing coloured water
and gulal on each other singing, dancing and
beating the drums. Everyone makes merry
while some drink. This continues till noon
when they take their baths and change. The
villagers prepare rot kus and take them with
pulses and ghee shakkar. In the evening the
villagers assemble near the Shiva temple and
have a bonfire symbolic of the cremation of
Holika.
Laul-ka-Mela-This fair is celebrated at
Seoni proper where the devta from Mandhol
is brought. Shops and stalls are set up near
the ground where the dev/a dances. Merrygo-rounds are also set up. People from Shakrori
generally reach Seoni in the afternoon, wearing colourful dresses. On their way back they
take glass bangles. sweets. cloth and odds and
ends for children.
Rakhri (Rakhl)-Rakhri is celebrated in
Sawan when sisters tie rakhi round the wrists
of their brothers. Purohits also tie this round
the wrists of their jajmans. ,People take rich
meals consisting of ghee and shakkar, and
dress up well. Brothers visit their sisters'
houses and offer them presents. Brahmans

also celebrate this day for vyas puja or guru
puja and change their sacred thread (yagyo
pavit). They also write Ram Ram il! small
rectangular spaces near the door posts and
decorate it with a mauli.
Digaili-It is believed by the villagers, that
in the month of Bhadon the devtas leave their
usual sthans and assemble at a common rendezvous. which gives art opportunity to the
evil spirits to occupy the places left by the
devtas. The villagers daily burn incense at the
deothans and in the evening the drummers
beat on their instruments to scare av'/ay the
evil spirits. The villagers further believe that
there are two days in Bhadon when the evil
spirits are more powerful than usual and,
therefore, they take further precautionary
measures against them. On these days (worked out by the pandit) people prepare dhindare
(Pataur) by frying rolled leaves of garhyaU
(arum colocasia) after dipping them in gram
flour. They cut a dhindara into four pieces
and throw them away from all the four cor-.
ners of their houses. They also place the
dratis (sickles) in the obra to save their cattle
wealth from the evil effect of the spirits.
lanam Ashtmi-Birth of Lord Krishna is
celebrated on this day and a fast is observed.
In the evening the people sing bhajans in Lord
Shiva's temple and after that they take food
prepared with ogla cholai alongwith milk and
fruit. This food is called phulwar (phalahat).
Some men remain awake whole of the night
singing bhajans in praise of Shri Krishna and
at mid-night a hymn or prayer is held.
Haritalika-In Seoni and nearby villages
Haritalika or Chirian Teeyan ka tcoliar, mainly a festival of girls and women belonging to
the Vaish community, is celebrated on the
third day after the Amavas of Bhadon every
year. It is a popular belief that unmarried
girls who worship Shiva on this day will get
good husbands while the married women will
lead long and prosperous lives and will never
be separated from their husbands. They wake
up early in the morning before the day dawns
and after bathing themselves they take s,-"cets,
bananas and milk. The whole of the day_
they would ~en observe fast and would eat
only on the morning of the next day after
offering water to Lord Shiva.
The whole of it is a gay festiva:I and the
women attired in colourful dresses gather together near the baC?lis, where they. make clay
images of Lord Shlva and ParvatI and also

SOCIAL AND CULrtJRAL LIFiJ

some toys. (The toy peacock on the cover of
this monograph is also reproduced from one
of the toys made on this occasion). Making
of these toys is very simple and requ.ires no
tools nor any great skill. Briefly, the clay
which is available nearby, is first kneaded into
a thick dough, by mixing water in it. This
dough forms the main material of the toys.
The dough is cut into small pieces of which
different shapes representing parts of the body
are made. These .parts are then joined together with the help of match sticks. When
the toys are half dried under the sun, they are
given a priming coat of peech (water in which
rice has been boiled), and this serves as a base
for applying colours to the toys.
In the evening they perform puja of Lord
Shiva after which the married women give
baya-five or seve,n sweetened and fried
loaves (mathis)-to their husbands, who give
them some money or presents according to
their financial capacity. The toys are then
given to the children for playing.

Dussehra-Dussehra in Bhajji has always
been celebrated with a difference. Unlike the
conventional functions connected with it all
over India no effigies of Rawan or Kumbhkaran are burnt here. Instead the villagers go to
Sedni where the devtas are brought from the
villages of MandhoI and Chhotabal, and spend
a day of gaiety.

Like other festivats of shakrori which are
celebrated at Seoni. the people gather there
in the afterno~m and watch the oracles getting
into a trance (hingarna) while four persons
carrying the devtas' palanquin dance in slow
steps. People offer money to the devtas and
make queries about their pains and worries
from the grehtas (oracles). As usual, the men
and women are present in colourful dresses
and spend on sundries like bangles, toys, immitation ornaments and sweets. Wine lovers
find it an opportune time to quench their
thirst, and sometimes local quarrels between
drunkards also take place. In the evening a
kariala or a drama may be staged at Seoni and
many persons remain awake throughout the
night watching this. Till recently, the rulers
of Bhajji also visited the festival ground.
SEASONAL FESTIVALS

The seasonal festivals celebrated in the
village are Lohri, Baisakhi, Nai-Roti-ka-Teohar, Booti-ka-Teohar and Maal. Except Lollri which is celebrated in a big way at Tattapani, all the other fairs and festivals are held
i.n the village.

Lohri-Lohri is celebrated on the first of
Magh every year when the winter is at its
peak. Early in the morning the people take
their baths and give alms of rice, pulses and
salt. After this they go to Tattapani where
about 4,000 persons gather to celebrate the
fair. Shops and stalls are also opened where
sundries and sweets are sold. As usual the
wine lovers drink at Seoni and stagger back
home in the evening.
Baisakhi-Baisaklii i3 celebrated on the 1st
of Baisakh every year and represents the harvesting of rabi crop. People take their bath in
the morning and give alms of chUru
(sweetened thin leaves of wheat flour) aI)d
some money by putting them on pitcher locally called mangtoo. Their meals ~onsist of
rice, pulses, ghee shakkar and Ch.ll!u, after
which they go to Seoni where deltles, and
villagers from many places spend a gay evening together. In the past the rulers also used
to attend.
Nai-Roti.ka-Teolzar-After the wheat crop
is harvested in the month of Baisakh and before the flour from the new crop is used a
small ceremony known as kanya pujan is ~erformed in which a meal is served to unmarned
girls and two rotis are given to a cow ..

46

SOCiAL AND CULTURAL LiFE

Booti-ka-Teohar-In
Asarh (June-July),
when the transplantation work of the paddy
is finished, it is customary among the villagers
to bring home a few of the seedlings to affix
to their door posts. The persons from other
households who helped in the transplantation
work are served with meals consisting of rice
and mithi dal.
Maal-This is celebrated a few days after
Dussehra. On this day flower garlands are put
round the necks of the cows and colours applied on their bodies. Oil is massaged on their
horns and then puja is performed. After this
the cows are sent to the jungle for grazing.
In the evening they are served with dala consisting of barley, rice and maize. No ploughing is done on this day.
Satya Narain-ki-katha
The villagers, especially the swarns also
perform kathas in their houses, by calling a
purohit from Shakra or Madhor. Arrallgements for katha are made in byoon-a room
in the ground floor, which .is cleaned and
plastered with cowdung for the occasion.
The purohit places the images of Ganesh,
Laxmi, Durga and other gods (if available
with the household) in one corner of the room
and dec_orates the place with banana leaves.
He also prepares shant representing navgrehas
with wheat flour. In a bronze plate (thali)
rice, betel nut, flowers and mauIi are also
placed. The person who arranges the katha
keeps fast.
The pandit then recites katba in a sing song
manner, by chanting the Sanskrit shalokas
from a text. The katha is told in five parts,
each part consisting of a short story showing
the importance of the katha. These stories also
tell about the punishments inflicted· on those
who fail to fulfill their vows or else who refused to receive the nived (prashad) of the
katha. After each part, the purohit rings the
bell and the person who arranges the katha,
blows conch shells. In the end arti is performed which is followed by circulating of a thalu
among the devotees who drop a coin or two
in it. All this money plus the dakashana given
by the household go to the purohit. Thereafter
the prashad (nived) is distributed among
them. The purohit also applies lilak to the
members of the family for' whom he recited
the katha.

Entrance to the Shall Temple
Navratras
Navratras come twice every year-once in
Chaitra and again before Dussehra. In Seom
Sub-Tehsil, the navratras of Shalli are very
famous and villagers from Shakrori and adjoining villages go there during these days.
There is also very old and famous temple of
Durga Bhagwati there. Many suffering villagers stay in the temple for all the nine days of
navratras and arrange chandi path in praise
of Shakti Durga. On the ninth day havlln is
performed and goats are sacrificed and offered
to Durga Bhagwati. Villagers believe that
during these days Durga manifests herself
in nine forms. Arrangements for the stay and
meals of pilgrims near the temple of Shalli
are made by local people. In the past there
used to be a large gathering but for the last
6-7 years, the number of pilgrims has been
waning gradually.
Beliefs in Spirits and Unseen Powers
There is a common belief in the existence of
spirits and imaginary powers. There are different sthans where spirits of Dano, Hanuman,
Sir and Bhagwati reside. The villagers, believe
that in • Bhadon (August-September) the
benign spirits leave their sthans to meet at a
common place for holding discussions for the
. well-being of their people. The evil spirits try
to bCcupy the sthans· temporarily left by them.
Therefore, the villagers burn incense in these
sthans as a precaution to keep away. the evil
spirits. They also believe that the evil spirits
exercise power from evening to the midnight
and after that the benign spirits drive them
away. One generally avoids going to lonely
places mostly those which are believed to be
haunted during the night.

SOCIAL AND CUL ruRAL liFE

Social Taboos
During menstruation period, the women are
not allowed to enter the kitchen or cook food,
and keep aloof during the period in one of the
corners of lower storey. No one touches them
and if any person does it by mistake he has
to take a bath to purify himself.. Women
are considered to be unclean for six days, after
which they take a bath and wash all the
clothes worn during the period.
Superstitions
The superstitions control the social struc~
ture to a great extent. For agricultural
operations an auspicious moment is selected
by the purohit and accordingly sowing and
ploughing are started. Villagers consider it
inauspicious to do any work during Bhadon
(August-September). It is considered good to
sow on Tuesdays, Wednesdays, Fridays and
Saturdays and to reap the harvest on Sundays,
Mondays and Thursdays. In case there are
two sittas (cobs) together on maize stalk or
wheat stalk it is considered inauspicious and
puja is performed while the surrounding crop
is given to the poor in charity. If any snake or
rat is killed while ploughing the land it is considered inauspicious. Havan is performed at
that place and a goat is sacrificed. While
sheering a sheep or a goat, water is sprinkled

41

on it in the name of devta which is known as
binnana.
If jackals howl in the day or a crow cries
at night, people become suspicious and fear
that some mishap might occur. It is also considered unfavourable to find iron on the way.
The falling of a tree is also considered unpropitious. People are suspicious of removing a
dead snake through the door and instead they
take it out through chhappar (roof). The down
of a crow is also considered inauspicious.
There is a deep belief in the evil eye. The
people are cognizant that certain persons are
~vil-eved and whenever they happen to stare
at their cattle. they dry up and do not yield
milk. A bull with eve mud is also avoided.
A dry tree before a house is not at all a good
omen since it is a sign of barrenness.
The first child born in Kartik is considered
- unlucky and parents perform a shanti pujan.
They consider it good to get their hair cut
either on Monday or on Wednesday. and undertake journeys either on Sunday or on
Thursday depending upon the direction in
which they are to go. Many households hang
earthen pots full of water on one of the tree
branches near their houses during summer for
thirsty birds.

5 Kariala and Folk Songs
K A R I A L A is a local word ascribed to a folk drama which originated in
some of former princely states of Himachal Pradesh. Here some seven or
eight men irrespective of their caste and age dress up as girls and put on
feminine robes. We have not seen any women taking part. It is ail assortment
of variety shows and has been popular among the masses from olden times.
The main cause of its popularity is that in the rural areas of Himachal
Pradesh there is no facility of modern entertainment. There is no set dialect
in which the kariala is performed, but it is worded in such a way as to
make the audience cheery. The greater part of the show is worded in a local
dialect and to make it more attractive and joyful by some sort of an assorted
mixture of HindI, Urdu, Punjabi or Pahari.
Karialchis-The persons who take part in this performance form a
special group who are professional in this trade and are commonly known
as karialchis. The karialchis o[ Shakrori or Dhami are known for their performance. They go to Rampur, Theog, Solon and Kasumpti tehsils and sometimes they may collect as much as two hundred rupees in a single night excluding the food which is given free by the villagers. As a better part of dialogue, the karialchis have a tendency to speak in verse and this primitive form
of drama and dance is full of very witty puns which excite huge guffaws of
laughter. A good many of them are, however, indecent. The more naughty they
are, the greater is the applause among the audience who clap humorously
away and pass remarks among themselves. Very often kariala is performed
nearabout Diwali when the harvest is complete and when there are a few
holidays. These make it a most welcome feature. Sometimes it comes as a
manauti. They may have a very crudely built up akhara with four banana
plants dug in the corners and pipal leaves, marigold flowers and mamw
leaves hanging like buntings, or some other contrivance for a stage. The nearest house bocomes the green room.
Musical instruments-There are a few fixed musical instruments which
are used and the taals are also set ones. Generally dhol, harmonium, kamal,
nagara and slzahnai form the orchestra. The taals are jung, kaihrva and dadra.
The karialchis do not deviate from this system of performance. The approximate cost of the musical instruments is about Rs. 200 to 300'.
Kariala of 1947 and now-Recalling the kariala, seen at Seoni'many years
ago of which some of us have vivid recollections and after seeing this show
at Shakrori we feel that this kind of a folk drama has hardly undergone any
change. Near the central place adjoining the temJ?I~, childr~n's park and
the big pipal tree, we heard one of the local mUSICIanS playmg a popular
though slightly old film song on the harmonium-Dil lutne wale. A_s soon
as we settled down, the musicians took their places and soon the VIllagers
collected.

of Lord Krishna.

and about the karialchis.

6 Education
TAB L E XI reveals that there are 27 persons 05 men and 12 women) in
the village who are just literate; they can read and write a simple letter. ,53
persons (45 men and 8 women) have completed primary school education. 9
men and 3 women are matriculates. One man is an intermediate while 3 teachers posted outside. are matriculates and trained teachers. This brings the total
number of literate persons in the village to 96 (73 males and 23 females) i.e.
27 per cent of the popUlation in the village is literate. This percentage
literacy is very satisfactory as compared with the 19 per cent literacy of
the whole of Mahasu District. The literacy is expected to increase in coming years as almost all children of school going age attend the school with
some exceptions which are common everywhere.
Primary School Shakrori
There is a Primary School in the village started in 1961. where boys
and girls are taught together. The students on roll, at the time of investigation were sixty-six-thirteen boys and eight girls in the 1st class; twelve
boys and eighteen girls in the 2nd class; three each in the 3rd. two each
in the 4th and three boys and two girls in the 5th class.
Till the summer vacation of 1962 there was only one teacher attached
to the school. Now. however. one more trained teacher has joined. Administratively and also for primary examination the school falls under the
jurisdiction of Centre School Basantpur. The annual examinations of lower
classes are conducted in the school by a teacher authorised by the District
Inspector of Schools.
School Building-The children often have open air classes under shady
trees during summer and in the sun in winter. The villagers have now constructed a school building with a subsidy of Rs. 1,600 obtained frof!l the
Government and a similar amount by way of oontributions (cash and I or
kind or labour). The school building consists of a big room about IS' xIS'
and· a verandah. The furniture is in the shape of some chairs. a table and
a blackboard. The boys and girls sit on the ground. either in the children
park constructed by the Community Development Block. Kasumpti or in
the open fields.

Government Higher Secondary School, Seoni
The Government 'Higher Secondary School at Seoni started as a Primary
School in 1913, was raised to Middle Standard in 1929. It was again raised
to high Standard in 1947 and to Higher Secondary Standard in 1961. The
school has about 500 students and a staff of 17 teachers, There are about
20 students from Shakrori who rome daily. Boarding facilities are provided

48

EDUCATION

for about 80 students or so. The school has h
junior division of the N.C.C.. one troop of
A.C.C. and another troop of 16 scouts. Training is imparted in the first aid and home nursing. There is a fairly big play-ground. The

barnes played in the school are Foot Ball,
Hockey, Volley Ball, Badminton and Basket
Ball. The academic results have been. on the
whole. satisfactory.

7 Medical
S H A K R 0 R I is fairly clean in winter, but is inclined to get dirty during
the monsoons. There are no drains. Narrow ill-paved village lanes, plenty
of flies and insects are found. Houses are swept daily although the dirt is
thrown in the adjoining fields. Houses are becoming cleaner and tidier. Villagers have a tendency to keep small windows, chimneys in the kitchens.
cleaner kitchens 'and slightly bigger cowsheds are seen in the newer ,built
houses. Once or twice a year~ the houses are washed by using local mitti
or makol which gives a very attractive colour. They prefer to use mitti because this. is available locally. Moreover they do not have to put in an
effort for golu or some other form of white-washing. The village has been
covered by the National Malaria Eradication Programme and had encouraging results. The staff regularly visits village during monsoons and in summer
and spray DDT in the houses.
Prevalent Diseases
Cold, cough. dysentery. diarrhoea and pneumonia are the diseases one
hears of in the area. Malaria was once prevalent in the village but not now.
because of the National Eradication of the Malaria Campaign in the Pradesh,
this- has, to a very great extent, vanished. Now the Malaria section of the
Medical Department of Himachal Pradesh has started malaria survei1lance
programme in the village, according to which they test the blood of eve!y
sick person to see if it contains any malarial germs. In case any malanal
germs are found, he is treated immediately.
Medical Care
In 1961-62 fourteen maternity cases were recorded and all of them were
confined at home, without the assistance of a dai. For simpler ailments the
villagers have developed faith in allopathy and 54 persons were treated with
allopathic medicines. They also believe in the ayurvedic treatment at least
for sil1}ple troubles but in complicated cases they prefer to have an allopathic
treatment. Besides, they have some indigenous treatments for troubles like
rheumatism. There is a dispensary at Chaba and also an indoor Govt. hospital at Seoni, where minor operations are carried out. There is also a
Mobile Unit. All this is of great help.
Vaid's Prescription
An old Vaid practises at Shakrori. Some of the medicines prescribed
by the 70 years old Vaid are given at the end of the chapter.
Indigenous Treatment of Rheumatism
One out of .the multitude of tra~1itional treatments bordering each craft,
much prevalent III many parts, espeCIally the remoter localities, is the extrac-

MEDICAL

Having treatment
tion of blood through sucking by means of a
horn. This is considered efficacious in cases of
rheumatism or prolonged inflammation.
Roughly speaking the treatment takes the following course:When a patient of rheumatism or inflammation, either of his own accord or due to
recommendations of his well-wishers, especially
those who have experience, decides to get relief
from the ailment by extracting of blood, he
either calls to his house the person who is
expert in the treatment of blood sucking or
himself repairs to the house of such a person.
He shows the ailing part of his body to the
expert. After feeling the area of complaint by
pressure of fingers the expert tries to locate
the point which is aching most. A massage
with oil or any other greasy substance is then
applied to the ailing part.
The expert is already equipped with a few
horns, hollowed as they are. The upper-most

end which is the thinnest portion has been
smoothly removed so as to allow the air to
run. through it. On this small hole is placed
a pIece of transparent clothlike material (Cob
web or coopra grass) in order to prevent the
blood to pass into the mouth of the sucker.
Thus prepared, the horn is fixed on the specific ailing part, pressed in a little, the expert,
by means of his mouth, draws out air in the
hor,n, with considerable force. This process,
after the small quantity of air in the horn has
been drawn out, causes the part of the body
to distend into a ball. The expert then removes
the horn and with the help of a blade or razor
or any other sharp instrument makes a few
small cuts on the distended ball of the body.
He then again applies the horn and sucks
heavily thus bringing blood out of the cuts.
The process, if fails to produce the desired
results, is again gone through. The treatment
is knowll as 'Singi Lagana' or 'Maila Kadna',

8 Eeono.my
THE main rural economy here whirls around agriculture. 95 per cent of
the families own land and cultivate it. The only families who do not possess
land in Shakrori are a Sood and three Lohars. The people do not believe in
leasing lands to others. and except in some stray cases. the owners till it
themselves. Besides agriculture. shoe making, black-smithy. carpentry,
basketry and shop keeping are also practised by some of the households as
their major occupation. There are four Brahmins and five Rajputs who are
in service either at Chaba Power Station or else in the Himachal Pradesh
Administration. and whatever land they possess in the village they plough
it themselves by taking leave from their office or by hiring labourers on
daily wages for the purpose. Their women folks then do the rest of the work
of looking after weeding and harvesting the crops.
The subsidiary occupation is""':labour on road construction work or in
kattha extraction. The male adults are engaged as labourers when they are
free from their agricultural operations. Running of water mills like glzarats and
paddy husking mill provide yet another occupation to some. The ladies are
often seen passing leisure time in spinning and making manjris (mat). Blacksmiths, carpenters. basket makers and shoe makers belong to scheduled
castes only.
Income and Expenditure

.Income-Table XII shows that about 50 per cent of the households
come under the income group of more than hundred rupees per month.
-Three persons in the income group of less than twenty-five rupees are, in
fact, widows living separately as single member households. Their holdings
are small and are cultivated for them by some of their relatives. These ladies
subsist entirely on the produce of their land and have no other source of
income. Food grains produced are mostly consumed at house while only a
small portion is exchanged for other necessaries like spices. salt.· tobacco
and clothes.
Out of the fifty-two families inves6gated, forty-two depended mainly
upon agriculture though, during off seasons the men folks would work as
labourers on road construction work or with kattha dealers.
No harijan has a monthly income of more than a hundred rupees. Out
of the ten Lohars contacted only four could find place in the income group
of Rs. 76-100 and that too very recently as a result of road constr;tction
work near the village. Till recently, the harijans were mostly servmg as
menials. and had little or no land of their own. Even. now when they have
been allotted nautor lands on payment of a nominal price. or :zazrana. to
the Government, their holdings are very small and also of an mf~nor quahty,
which results in low yield. They have to depend more on workmg on roads
than on their fields.

. Expenditure-The amounts spent by the
VIllagers on food, clothing, education Rnd other
items in their respective income groups may
now be discussed. Other items include expenditure on travelling, recreation, fuel and

Table XUI reveals that except the households in the income group of over Rs. 101 and
more the percentage expenditure on food
gets less and less as income increases. The

trend indicated by the other income groups.

households in the income group of Rs. 101
and above are joint families having a large
number of members in each of them ..

Indebtedness
The villagers claim that formerly when
there was no motor road and when the people
were entirely dependent on agriculture, they
were under the debts of nearby village shopkeepers. I t is only after the merger of the
States and the emergence of Himachal Pradesh that some of the persons were able to
get jobs in Simla and nearabout and repaid
their debts_ They still get loans from sources
like local villagers and shopkeep.:!rs_ But they
repay this debt in convenient instalments after
every harvest or on getting financial help from
a member of their family working at Simla.
At the time of investigation in 1961 the total
amount of debt outs.tanding with the villagers
worked out to about two thousand rupees.

Inheritance of Property
Property may be acquired either by inheritance or by purchase. In the past, on the dea th
of a father the son or sons became equal
share holders of the deceased's property. In

case the deceased had no sons, the property
was inherited by the brothers. His wife had
only life interest while the unmarried daughters were to be maintained by the beneficiary
till they were married. Now there have been
changes in the inheritance law and accordingly the property is divided in a manner in
which one part of it is inherited by widow
or the widows jointly and the rest goes to J1is
sons and daughtcrs equally. A man who had
2 wives at the time of his death, 3 sons and
one daughtcr by one wife and 2 sons by the
other and had 42 bighas of land. the property
would be divided into 7 parts (one for two
wives, and one each for every son and daughter). This means the widows. jointly would
have 6 bighas 3 bighas each Nhile every son
and every daughter will have equal shares of
6 bighas. The entries are made in the revenue
records even though with their shaking economy in view or for any other reason the
brothers and, sisters and their mothers plough
the land jointly and live together as one house·
hold_
Generally, the daughters do not ask for
their shares from their parental property and
say so while filling in an affidavit in the court.
Only very few persons agreed that the daughters should inherit property equally with the
sons.
Shop Keeping
For more than fifty years a Sood from
Kangra is running a shop_ He has no children and has adopted a Brahmin, from the
neighbouring village_ Till recently there was
only one shop but now one more shop has
opened since December. 1961.

instead of having these things from Seoni,
where their children go daily to attend the
school. This is because they can have a sort
of a barter with the local shopkeeper. This
way the shopkeeper gains two folds. firstly by
charging higher prices for the goods and
secondly by paying local price for the foodgrains Which he purchases. The shopkeepers
charge more because they have to cater to
limited people and have to get many consumable goods on permit from Simla or from
Seoni and they sell them next to the house.
Thus it is seen that the foodgrains are generally collected by the shopkeepers locally
while. the other commodities they generally
bring from Simla.
The method of transaction is mostly barter
but money also 'changes~ hands. Since the
shopkeepers deal in variety of substances they
run the business throughout the year and
there is no specific time or reason connected
with their commerce.

Water Mills
Gharats-The water mills (Gharats) arc
owned by 3 households and run by the flow
of water from the nallah. They are there for
the last 20 years or more. A wooden channel
leads the gushing water into the base of the
gharat where the wooden charkar.ies are fixed
into an iron socket. These rotate by the force
of the flowing water and the grinding stone
also rotates. Wheat grains are poured from
the top into a hopper. For everyone maund
of grains ground into aUa 2 seers of flour is
charged and deducted from the out turn. In
the past these gharats remained open but now
they are locked. so that no pilfering takes
place .•

Paddy Husking Mill-This mill has been
started only recently by a villager from Nalyanj (12 miles). During the paddy season it
starts w'orking while during the ·off season.
the machine is removed. The owner charges
2 seers of dhan for everyone maund of dhan

ECONOMY

pounded. This year he intends to put on some
accessories to this mill so that the mill may
be ~ade to do cotton ginning and allied jobs
dUrIng the off season. The villagers can now
save some time by taking their produce to the
mill instead of pounding them at home with
the conventional ookhli and 11100/a.
Manufacture of Kattha
Kattha, used in betels, is extracted from
khairwood which is chisseled into small pieces
and put in the "andies containing water. These
hafldies are then placed on the hearth in a
row. The wooden pieces get dissolved in water
and form a paste which dries up when cooled.
Many villagers are employed by the contractors in the working season, from December to
March. and this influences their economy to
a great extent. In 1962, the hearths were set
up at Dattyar about four furlongs away and
ten persons from Shakrori were employed as
labourers who got wages at two rupees per
day.

9

Agriculture
A G R I ~ U L T U R E of the olden times still continues to be the mainstay of
Shakrori and about 95 per cent of the families are engaged in this profession.
Table XVIII abstracted from the land utilisation statement from the village
note book, classifies the soils of the village from 1951-1960.
The cultivated area during all these years has remained static nor has there
been any increase in the area under irrigation. There is no land under
forests.
The soil is sandy loam in texture,' abounds in pebbles and is not very
deep. For finding out the avail abIes of nitrogen phosphorus, potash and pH
in the different types of soils in Shakrori, the Soil Testing Department, Himachal Pradesh took their surface samples, and the results are given in Table
XIX for comparison, which shows that except .for potash which is low in irrigated and nautor lands, rest of the availables are normal in all the types of
soil in the village. pH (potential Hydro-ioJ;ls) is neither acidic nor alkaline.
The villagers make up the deficiency of potash by rotation of crops in the
irrigated fields while only one crop is reaped froquhe nautor land.

Crops and Calendar
The crops grown in the village and their calendar showing crop seasons,
duration of crops and agricultural operations are given in Tables XX to
XXII.
Major Operations of Principal Crops
PADDY

The different varieties of rice grown here are Ratwa. Rorha and Begum
which are being produced from the olden times. Dehra Dun Basmati is also
very popular but because of great labour involved in its cultivation, the villagers sow it in small quantities. Rec~ntly an improved variety of rice '(.21
has also been introduced and it is believed to have given very satisfactory
results. More and more people are now changing over to the improved
variety.

Preparation of soil--The work connected with paddy cultivation starts
in Jeth (April-May) when the cultivators repair terraces and water the fields
demarcated for growing seedlings. The fields are puddled and given four
ploughing before they are considered fit for the sowing. After every ploughing the puddled fields are levelled with the help of phalli (an agri~ultural
implement which is just like suhaga but has a sharp' edged plank mstead)
until it turns into a thick paste, in which the workers 'sink up to their knees.
The manure is spread on the fields after they are given a third ploughing .
......-:::..........a.;::;;..c::;;;;--- The leaves of bainshti also serve as' a good manure and are liberally used.

Sowing and transplantation-Before sowing, the seed is first soaked in a pitcher for
three days after which it is drained and put
in a tokni for another few days. This germinated dhan is then sown by broadcast. It
takes one month for the seedling to be transplanted. The transplantation work is done in
a team and many households combine to sow
seedlings~singing away in the pouring rains.
The crop does not require much looking
after once it is transplanted, except that the
fields are watered every tenth day regularly
and weeding is also done thoroughlyHarvesting-Paddy harvest begins in October, when the crop is cut with the sickles and
their small bundles or muthris are arranged
in the fields for drying. This is known as
Sathri lagana. The muthris dry after 3 ot 4
days, then they are brought near the khalyan
(threshing floor) where they are heaped into
big racks or kunnus, and covered with straw
as a precaution against any rains. After all
the paddy has been collected in kunnus it is
then spread in the khalyan for separating t~e
dhan from the straw or paral. The khalyan IS

invariably cleaned and plastered with clay and
cowdung at every harvest. The threshing is
done either by bullocks who tread on the
muthris in the khalyan or else the muthris are
beaten with large bue! sticks. The Block has
provided a paddy thresher for demonstration
and some five or six families use this machine
regularly. The dhan is then collected separately. Winnowing is done by throwing il on a
clean place from wicker baskets when 'wind is
blowing. For paddy husking. the age old
method in the village is the use of ookhli moot
and the people still use if for smaller quantities which are consumed in the house. Recently a villager from Nalyani has also installed
a husking mill run by water and he charges
twb seers of dhan per maund.
MAIZE

Next to paddy in the kharif season is maize,
which is planted in the barani fields as also in
the nautor land in the last week of May (or the
beginning of June), after a few showers. The
baran; land laid out in neat little plots--a few
feet squares and separated by paths, does not
require much preparation for the crop. except
that the terraces are repaired and the fields are
cleared of stones and chyool (thorns). The
varieties of maize sown are sathru, which
matures in sath or sixty days and sambhar.
Ganga 103 an improved variety has also been
grown by one household and the results are
satisfactory.
Sowi_ng-The maize is also sown by broadcast and' for fair distribution of the seed the
villagers divide a plot into four. six or eight
sections or hal/ais according to the size of the
field and scatter the seed in aU of them. The
first ploughing and levelling is done onl.y aft~r
the seed has been broadcast. ManUrIng IS
done only in case there is a surplus frpm the
paddy fields.
When the crop is about si-> inches above
the ground, weeding is done with the help of
gaan (a wooden implement having some five
or seven teeth and"" run by bullocks). Hoeing
is also done with a khilni. Second ploughing is
then done"very carefully keeping in view that
no crop is uprooted.
Harvesting-The crop ripens sometime in
Bhadon. It is reaped with sickles and brought
near the khalyan, where cobs are removed
and put in the sun for drying after which
the grain is threshed out ~f the cobs, by bc~ting them with buel stIcks, and stored III
perus.

December

The kuhli and barani soils require different
treatment before the wheat is sown. The
kuhli fields are puddled with water after the
first ploughing so as to melt all the clods in it
while the latter is simply ploughed and levelled. The second ploughing in the barani' land
is done within a fortnight after its first ploughing but in the kuhli fields it is done sometime
in October-November.
Sowing-The seed. as is the practice with
the sowing of all other crops. is scattered by
broadcast and then the fields are ploughed
and levelled. It requires about 8 path as or 16
seers of seed to sow one bigha of land.

cnds.
Threshing of paddy.

The crops grow up about six to nine inches
by December-January when the fields are
manured,. The manuring or gabrain is done
in a team in which one person from every
household joins hands with others and manure
a particular lot of fields in turns. This is
reciprocal. The crop requires quite a lot of
weeding and this is done by the women regularly.
In case there are insufficient winter rains.
the villagers irrigate their kuhli land under the
crop in February.
Harvesting-The reaping is mostly done
by the ladies and in Baisakh when the grains

63

AGRICULTURE

are fully grown and matured the trop sheaves
are spread in line in the fields (Painti Lagami).
While the men carry the muthris or bundles
near the khalyan for threshing and winnowing. All the muthris collected here are
kept in heaps or kunris before they are arranged in the khalyan in such a manner asto keep all the ears directly facing in the sun.
This process of arranging the khalyan laona.
The threshing is done with the help of bullocks who are made to tread on the stalks,
after which the grain is separated from the
straw by winnowing with a wicker basket. This
is known as punana. The grains are stored in
perus or wicker casks kept in one of the corners of the living room. The straw is slored
separately for consumption by the cattle.
PEAS

Peas is a cash crop which does not require
much labour and yet yields an average income
of about two hundred rupees in a season. It
takes four months-Maghar to Chet for the
peas to ripen.
The fields do not require any preparatory
tillage or treatment befora the seed is sown.
After the seed is scattered by broadcast
method. first ploughing and levelling is done
and the fields are also manured. The plants
do not require any care after sowing except
that they are provided supports to keep them
ttailing on the fields.
Aft~r the peas are grown, the villagers remove the pods and sell them as green peas at
Simla, and whatever quantity is left unsold is
used as seeds or else consumed at home,·
~

GROUNDNUT

The gtoundnut is mixed with the maize and
sown by broadcast. There is no extra labour
over and above what is done for the maize.
Thus the ground nuts cost the villagers nothing
except the price of the seed . ./ The crop is ripe
after a month of reaping maize when the nuts
are dug out.
,The harvesting is done by the .school children who-daily bring home small quantities of
the ground nut after digging them out from
their fields. Whatever quantity is produced,
is consumed in the house.

Besides the crops, the fields also provide
fodder for the cattle and also roofing material
for the thatched roofs of the obras. The

Horticulture

There is a Government progeny orchard at
Seoni with an area of 108 bighas, which was
started by the Agriculture Department in
1957. It has grown many varieties of lemon.
guava, loqllat, pomegranates, mango, lichi,
grape-fruit. malta and oranges. The mallas
and oranges have been. however, a failure in
a~ much as the fruit is very small and sour,
and this has discouraged the villagers from
growing these fruits.
The villagers have not very much changed
over to horticulture and have grown only some

AGRICVLTURE

64

fruit trees. Persimmon, pomergranates. jujufus,
lime, guava, plum, grape-fruit oranges, loquat,
jaman, lichi and bananas are grown in the
adjoining villages of Chaba, Matiogri, Begain
and Basantpur. Some of the villagers, who rjossess nautor land above the Simla-Tatapani
motor road have now started growing some of
these fruits. Villagers feel mango trees are
difficult to grow and that it takes years before
any results are achieved; papita is short lived
while guava requires a lot of looking after
against parrots during the days and against
flying bats during the night. These bats are
a perpetual chirping and infuriating little
birds who have lived in the neighbourhood
across the river-on shisham and old mango
trees-and who are a great nuisance since
decades.
Pests and crop diseases
There are many diseases from which tne
crops of the villagers suffer. The major diseases diagnosed and cured by the farmers
are:Malahi-A sort of insect which cuts the
roots of the wheat crop. At the time of first
plough the farmers collect the insect from the
field and throw it away in Sutlej. There is no
other effective control for this insect.
Mankharu-Small insect which cuts the top
leaves of paddy plants. They know 110 local
cure.
1hdla-A sort of disease to maize and mash
crop which results in drying up the leaves of
maize plant, which however, remains green.
As a result of this disease 1-th of the crop
fails.
.
Kungi-This disease is confined to wheat
crop. Wheat plants tum red and yield comparatively 25 per cent less wheat than the
usual yield. The disease is due to cloudy season.
There is another disease affecting chillies
and paddy, as a result of which chilli~s' and
paddy plants dry up and yield no fruit. No
name is given to this disease ..

also help the blacksmith in lighting the furnace
while the blacksmith makes the implements.
He is paid for his services by . way of anaj
(foodgrains). Besides, he also undertakes to

There is a distinct"change over what used to
be the marketing arrangements in the past.
Formerly the peasant was obliged to sell his
produce to his mahajan-a shopkeeper at
Seoni. The shopkeeper would always keep
his debtors in a position where they could be
exploited to the best advantage. Now, with
better yields and a better market, they sell
their produce like peas and rice, maize and
vegetables direct at Simla. While in certain
cases, some wandering shopkeepers from
Simla or other places go to Shakrori and purchase the produce at local rates which are no
doubt less than the market rates, yet better
than the rat!!s, the peasants would get from
the shopkeepers, at Seoni. The agents from
Simla generally purchase the foodgrains at
Rs. 3 to Rs. 5 per mauqd less than th(~ price
at Simla. This is because the peasants, are
sometimes pessimistic and think it better to
sell at their homes even for a lower price than
to go to Simla, taking all their produce there.
They say it would be uneconomic for them to
go and spend for their boarding and lodging
at Simla and then they might not get a fair
price for their goods after all.
Animal Husbandry
Live Stock,-We gather that villagers some-

times buy their buffaloes and cows from traders of Bilaspur and from fairs at Rupar.
These traders may bring calves and sell them
to the villagers whenever they come across
them. A pair ,of good desi bullocks may cost
them from Rs. 300 to Rs. 500 or Rs. 700 to
Rs. 800. A buffalo may cost them from
Rs. 300 to Rs. 500. We have not come across
any very good pair of desi buffaloes, but they

are pahari Nilli-buffaloes. The cows are
slightly bigger than what we have seen them'
in other villages, but their milk yield is poor.
Livestock with the villagers in Shakrori is
given in table XXV.
BufJaloes-There are only 2 desi buffaloes
(Gujaroo) as against 59 pahari. Villagers
generally purchase them from nearabout villages in Suket, Arki, Nalagarh and Bilaspur.
Desi or Gujaroo buffaloes have also been
bought from near Simla, where they had come
from the plains. The buffaloes generally start
yielding milk at the age of 3-!-4 years
and continue for about 8 to 9 years. Daily milk
yield per buffaloe being about 8 to 9 seers (Desi)
and 3 to 4 seers (Pahari). A desi buffaloe
costs from 500 to' 700 rupees while pahari
buffaloe costs from 200 to 300 rupees. The
cattle are given huel leaves, grass, and oilcakes and even chokar, but the villagers do
not form the systematic schedule about their
diet. They are also sometimes given salt. At
least one member from every household daily
wakes early in the morning and goes to Moongna (5 miles) to fetch grass for the cattle.
Goats-While going around some of these
homes, we came across a number of goats and
sheep. many of which have been bought from
the Kinnauras that go by, for ten to twelve
rupees. They are bought as kids and lambs
and are sometimes sold at a price between
Rs. 50 and Rs. 80 when grown up. These Kinnauras come from Asrang. Raurang, Kilba.
Duna, Pangi, Spillo and Sorang. The goats
supply wool for kharchas (goat hair mats) and
besides meat, they also provide· a good manure. Their rearing is cheaper than other livestock and there are ghasnis and scrub forests
which provide ample food for them. Even
landless labourers may also possess a few
goats. The goats and sheep sold by the Kinnauras are however, not of improved variety.
Kidding is about twice in fourteen months,
and high fertility always brings about an income to the owner. The bigger goats and
sheep are sold here, by the villagers, and not
the kids. It is likely that more people may
take to keeping goats and sheep though they
have a habit of nibbling the leaves of forests
and all varieties of plants, and are considered
a pest at times. We cannot eliminate goats
from rural economy. (The hair of Himalayan
goats find theiI: way with the Kinnauras).
Thousands and thousands have been trecking
along and passing these villages since by-gone
ages.

A Kinnaura family has been coming to
Shakrori and Seoni for the last 24 years. They
have their tremendous good-will in the village
and for those, in whose dhogris they stay, and
in whose fields they lodge for winter, they invariably bring shawls, chuli, chilgoza and, we
are not so sure, even anguri. The soft warm
fibre wool is not the best as one would expect.
It is slightly rough, but the villagers seem to
be satisfied with the goat, the sheep, the wool
and the finished products. It is quite common
to see young boys, young men, or old
women or young women beating all their cattle back from the spring or from around in the
grazing fields and in the scrub forests. They
are few with some families and, therefore,
they do not have a gaddi dog to look after
them. The goats are fed on leaves of kika"
ber, bushes of karonda, basuti and pipai
leaves.
There is no cattle-pound. So the fuming cultiyators settle around calmly when any of these
cattle stray across.
Either the cattle are
thrashed or the shepherd. Goat's milk is seldom sold. (The goat manure is more useful
than the cow manure, but it is mixed with
other manure and finally cast into the fields).

A veterinary Assistant Surgeon is incharge of
the hospital. The hospital attends to about 15
cattle daily. Most common cases detected by
the hospital are simple wounds, lousiness,
fever, bronchitis, indigestion, dyspepsia, diarrhoea and broken horns. From 1957 to 1962
there have been only 7 out-breaks of foot
and mouth disease but they were rapidly
checked from spreading.
For improving the breed of the cattle, the
hospital keeps one cow bull of jersey-sindhi
type and one murrah bufIaloe bull.
The poultry unit attached to the hospital
keeps improved white leg horn. Besides
giving demonstration to the villagers, the unit
also supplies eggs for hatching and for eating.
Poultry
The villagers possess about 40 to 50 birds.
It was only 3 or 4 years back that they started
taking some interest in poultry. Only two
households possess improved birds. A Drahman from the neighbouring village of Matiogri has, however. started a poultry farm on a
larger scale. He has some two hundred birds.
all of improved variety. People do not generally sen the birds for eating. but eggs are sold
by all who have them spare. The eggs at
Matiogri are sold at the rate of 3 annas per
egg, and good chicken costs about Rs. 6 to
Rs. 10 a bird.

10 Village Crafts
TIlE seasonal and "organic" character of agricultural production calls for
other occupations and pursuits to: fill the resulting periods of partial or total
inactivity. The most natural of these supplementary occupations were originally and, to a large extent, sill remain the transformation and finishing of
agricultural raw materials by the agriculturist himself. All the basic human
wants are provided in the household economy. Not only food, but principal
clothing and housing material are also prepared. We take to some of the villagers who do this.
Spinning
The few sheep owned by some households provide wool. This may also
be purchased from Kinnauras. After it has been teased with fingers and particles of twigs and leaves or any dirt, are removed, the wool is pulled out
from a matted state and kept iIi neat little baskets or chhikkus.
The spinning is done with taklu or spindle, which consists of a thin
round piece of hardwood, about ten inches long and a whirl or chhatr also
made of hardwood, attached to it some two inches from the bottom. For
spinning the wool is twisted to the desired thickness with the right hand
fingers and is tied round the dandi (shaft) of the taklu. Having drawn out
about two feet of wool and holding it with right fingers and thumb the taklu
is given a sharp twist. When the wool is well twisted, the taklu is stopped and
the spun yarn is wound on its shaft. The process is repeated till the taklu is
all full of the spun yarn.
Weaving
Villagers have to depend mostly upon a centinarian in the neighbouring village of Zedwin for weaving. The weaving is done on the pitlooms
which the weaver has made. The shuttle or nalu, containing thread for the
weft, in this type of Mom, is thrown across the warp by hand. Tlie loom is
installed/in a separate shed, which houses one more loom and is twelve
feet square.
The main material used for weaving is spun wool and is provided by
the customers. The dyed marino yarn for the designs is generally provided
by the weaver himself who purchases them from Kinnallras or from Seoni or
Simla. The designs on the pattus and cotton chaddars if at all there are any,
are geometrical. The most common are the checks for which the weaver does
not use any graph papers; he prepares the warp by counting the threads and
inserting dyed marino at regular intervals and for the weft he uses a small
match stick as a scale for using coloured yarn at appropriate places
Articles producedPatfu (a sort of blanket}-9 hmhs long and 3 haths wi~e,

Carpentry
One household is engaged in carpentry.
The carpenter belonging to Lohar family
generally remains out in Suket. where he
works as a builder. He makes rough door
frames and lays the roofing materiaJ. Sometimes he also makes wooden parts of the agricultural implements like ploughs and jungra for which he gets foodgrains at the rate of
one patha-2 seers. For house construction
work the carpenter is engaged on daily wages
of Rs. 3 per day plus meals for the whole
day. He earns about seventy rupees per month.

Shoe Making
There are two Chamars in the village and
both of them are engaged in shoe making
and agriculture. only one subsists mainly on
shoe-making. Mania Chamar has built his
house-cum-workshop in the main abadi and he
makes some better shoes. These are the pomp.
full sleepers, derby and do phite ki gurgabi.
Mania has named these shoes from old pattern book he possesses. Besides, he also repairs

VILLAGE CRAFTS

and resoles the shoes of his clients. He charges
Rs. 2.50 per pair for resoling but other ordinary
repairs are done free. The Chamar performs
two distinct services for his clients. Firstly, he
mends their shoes and secondly he undertakes
to remove their dead cattle. In lieu of this, Mania gets from his clients at every harvest:
3 pathas or 6 seers of foodgrains per
adult client who wears shoes.
1 binda or 4 seers of dhan or 1 muthri
of wheat at harvest.
Two maize chapaties and pulses on
every fair or festival celebrated in
the village.
Blacksmithy

Nandia the blacksmith is always seen working on a plough or a yoke or such other imple-

69

ments as are required by the villagers. Sometimes Nandia would make new implements but
mostly he is called upon to repair the older
ones. He is given 16 seers of anaj at every
harvest by all his clients per pair of bullocks
they possess, in lieu of which Nandia repairs
their implements and also sharpens their sickles. In case, however, new implements are to
be made, Nandia charges extra foodgrains, according to the labour involved in making them.
There is a distinct division of labour and when
Nandia is working on an implement, his client
is supposed to sit by his side blowing the furnace or hammering the red hot iron for annealing. The client also supplies wood and iron for
the implement and coal for the furnace while
the blacksmith would take out his tools and
starts making the implements.

Community Development andPanchayats
Community Development
N.B.S. block was started on 2nd October 1953 and converted into
C.D. Block with an allocation of12 lakhs on 1st April 1956. The period of
C.D. block stage for 3 years was extended by a year for the Kasumpti-Seoni
Block since it was not able to utilize its total allocation during the prescribed period. On 1st April 1960 the Block was converted into stage Il with
an allocation of Rs. 5 lakhs. The period of stage II will end on 31st March
1965 when the block will enter post-intensive stage.

THE

People's Attitude
The villagers are appreciative of what the Government has been doing
for them but in certain matters they are critical. _Rightly or wrongly. they
are of the opinion that much could be done if only the block officials were
more experienced; visited them oftener and patiently listened to their grievances.
. The children's park, just near the Shivalaya, is in a bad condition since
the villagers did not want the park. The responsibility for maintaining it
was also vested with the people through their Panchayat.
They have had advantages of a kuhl for many years before even the
Kasumpti-Seoni block was farmed. Now the kuhl has been made pucca. It
has been extended further at a cost of about Rs. 21,000. The water available
at the source Mohra Nal, was not sufficient to irrigate the whole of the
village. When, however, the villagers learnt that with the kuhl made pucca
they may also be levied some tax, they lost patience and started criticising
this new construction work. The portion of the kuhl, newly constructed, is
all lying unused and in a dilapidated condition. The villagers are sore about
this infructuous job.
Some villagers are allergic to the posting of raw hands who according
to them do not know about the conditions in the villages and thus do not
prove of any help to them. There is a general feeling that the block staff
seldom visit their village. If they do they are always in a hurry to leave. The
Gram Sewak for this village and the Gram Sewak of Sainj has been holding
dual charge.
Some are critical of the Agriculture Inspector because they were asked
to leave some plots vacant for demonstrations and none were given. A few
families say that the agricultural demonstrations have been given by the
block staff.
.
There is a general feeling among the villagers that experienced and
sympathetic officers; fully acquainted with their local hill conditions should

COMMUNITy DEVELOPMENT AND PANCHAYATS

have been posted in the block so that they
could have mature advice from them. Their
main grievance seems to be about block officials and lack of co-ordination of the different
activities carried out in the area.
Some villagers complain that they have not
been paid for the distribution of improved
seed of wheat, that they gave to their neighbours for sowing. According to them. they
feel. that they were entitled to a sum of one
rupee per maund.
With regard to veterinary service they were
fairly satisfied because of facilities available
at Seoni. They are also contented with Medical and Educational facilities. Their occasional criticism is about the personnel managing
these, who with more tact and courtesy could
do so much more to win good will and confidence of the people.
Panchayats
The Gram Panchayat came into existence
in 1954, and the Nyaya Panchayat started
functioning in 1957-58 after the Panchayat
Raj Act was passed in 1952-53. Both the Panchayats for the villages are located at Basantpur.
Shakrori is represented by Vice-President, a
Harijan member (reserved seat) and one swam
member in the Gram Panchayat, and by a
Naib-Sarpanch and a member in the Nyaya
Panchayat. There are in all 15 members in
each of these Panchayats and villages under

Elections
Last elections to Nyaya Panchayat and
Gram Panchayat were held in March and
August, 1962.
For Gram Panchayat elections, the Gram
Sabha area was divided into eight constituencies for unreserved seats, while the entire area
was considered as one constituency for the
election of a President, a Vice-President, re-

'n
served seats for women and Harijan candidates.
For the Nyaya Panchayat elections, the
Gram Sabha area was divided into 12 constituencies with Basantpur, Nadukhar and
Shakrori as double mem6er constituencies.
Elections to both the Panchayats were conducted by show of hands and the members
attended their meetings in May and September, last year.
Functions
The villagers know that Gram Panchayat
can:Bring about recpncili'ation between disputant parties;
Arrange to maintain village paths, recommena nautor land for grant, register births and deaths;
Recommend the grant of trees for construction of a house;
The Gram Panchayat also participates in
development works.
Nyaya Panchayat administers justice on all
civil and criminal cases under their jurisdiction and powers as laid down in section 67
(civil cases) and Section 57 read with Schedule I (Criminal cases).
The Pradhan or in his absence, the U p-Pradhan appoints "Samjhota Samiti" consisting of
five members for bringing about a compromise between disputant parties. The Samitis
do their best to settle the disputes by reconciliation, within a period of 3 months of receipt
of an application. If the reconciliation is not
yffected within that period, the case is transferred to the Nyaya Panchayat who try it in
one of their three benches consisting of a Sarpanch or Up-Sarpanch and four other members.

Vs. F.S. son of H.S., village 13asantpur.
(Case filed in the Gram Panchayat under

son of H.S. met me on the way, caught hold

of my neck, and started beating me. As a result, blood started oozing out from my neck

The applicant attached medical report from
the Medical Officer Seoni and also a report
from Police Station substantiating his wounds
as a result of beating.
Both KR. and F.S. were summoned by the
Samjhota Samiti on the 6th November, 1962
for reconciliation. Since the parties did not
want to compromise, the case was postponed

1962.
On 6th January 1963 both parties were
present and agreed to a compromise. The defendant agreed to pay five rupees to KR. as
medical charges and promised that he would
not do any harm to KR. again. The case was
then filed.

Cases dealt with by the Nyaya Panchayat
Case No. l-Shri K son' of A. Rajput
village Rejag Vs. Shri J. son of N.
village Shakrori.
Summary of the Case-K filed a civil
suit against one J of village Shakrori stating
that the latter owed him Rs. 67.25 nP. which
he had not paid till the filing of the suit. The
case was first dealt with by the Samjhota
Samitis for a period of 3 months but they
could not bring about any compromise between the two parties. Hence the plaintiff requested for transfer of the case to the Nyaya
Panchayat.
The case was received in the Nyaya Panchayat on the 20th December, 1960 and the
Sarpanch marked it to the Bench No.2 of the
3 benches. The Bench summoned both the
parties to appear on the 20th January 1961.
KR. after taking oath, stated that J owed
him Rs. 67.25 with interest. KR. did not pay
that money to J in cash. Albeit he gave 3 mds.
of wheat @ Rs. 22 per md. After that J was
constantly reminded for repayment of debt,
but all in vain. Hence he (K.R.) had to file a
civil suit against him.

J. however, in his reply admitted that he
owed the sum of Rs. 67.25 to the plaintiff, but
that he would not pay back the money till he
(J) did not get the sum of three hundred rupees
due from K.R. and others, which they owed
him against services rendered in the construction of a school bUilding. "As soon as these
persons pay me my money I shall re-pay the
sum of Rs. 67.25 to K R"
On questioning. he replied that three persons KR, J.D. and H.R owed him that sum

73

of Rs. 300 and that he could not file a suit
against them since he was a poor person.
J.R further stated that he was able to get
only sixty rupees against the construction of
school building and that too from the two persons. other than the plaintiff. The contract for
the construction of school building was drawn
by K.R. (plaintiff) himself and that J.R only
knew him at that time.
The bench decided the case on the same
day (20th January. 1961) in favour of K. R.
on the grounds that J. R had admitted that
he owed a sum of rupees sixty seven and
25 nP. to the plaintiff. A decree for Rs. 67.25
was ordered in favour of K.R The rate of inter(!st till final payment was fixed at 6 %.

ago."

of any witness. He said that KR went to his
house several times, but he never talked about
money.

that he owed him any money. he wanted the
'
case to be filed.

APPENDIX I
Records of the Geological Survey of India
Part I.

1939

March

Tbe Structure of Shali 'Window' near Simla.
By W.D. West, M.A. (Can tab), F.N.I. Geo.
logist, Geological Survey of India.
'

1. INTRODUCTION
The foundations of our knowledge of the
geology of this area
H. B. l'tledlicott
were laid by HB.
Medlicott
in
his
memoir "On the Geological structure and relations of the Southern portions of the Himalayan range between the rivers Ganges and
Ravee". published in 1864. In that work the
ShaH area provided him with a problem of
peculiar difficulty, in that less metamorphosed
rocks appeared to dip in aU directions beneath more metamorphosed rocks. Although
Medlicott was unable to solve the problem
himself. his observations on the geology were
so acute, and his recognition of the difficulties to be solved so clear, that it was possible.
after reading his description, and with a knowledge of the neighbouring area, to see the probable solution in advance. a solution that has
since been confirmed.
Medlicott covered the amazing area of 7.000
square miles in two seasons. striding like a
giant through the land. Had he been able to
examine - the geology in greater detail. it i_s
possible that he would final1y have come to
a satisfactory understanding of the structure,
though the theory of nappes, and the horizontal movement of large rock masses that they
imply, had not then been elaborated. As it
was, he was compelled to leave the problem
unsolved.
Subsequent to Medlicott's work, no geologist seems to have
H. H. Hayden
visited the area until
the late Sir Henry
Hayden came to. Simla as Director. On at least
three occasions during this period he spent
seven or eight days in camp on the southern
slopes of Nag Tikar. a peak north-east of
ShaH. The ebicf interest of these visits lay in
his discovery of Nummulitic beds on the
southern slopes of the ShaH mnge, a position unexpectedly far into the Himalaya for
beds of this age. This discovery was reported
in tbe General Report for the years 1918 and

1919.

As a consequence. of Hayden's discovery,
in the summer of 1920
R. W. Palmer
the late Capt. R. W.
Palmer was deputed to
survey the area in detail, and spent about six
weeks on either side of the Nauti khad. He
subseque~tly submitted a short Progress Report, whIch was accompanied by a map of
part of the area on the scale of two inches
to one mile. On this map Palmer showed a
number of small outcrops of Subathu beds
and he subdi~ided the rocks into variou~
groups: -Shah limestone. ShaH quartzite (a
stage that he subsequently a bandoped), Madhan slates, Subathu b~s, and the curiously
n.amed Mule Track senes (subsequently identdi~ br Pilgrim and West as their Chail
s~nes). In order of superposition upwards. In
hlS report be discusses the age of the Mule
Tra~~ series, and their apparently anomalous
pOSItion above less metamorphosed beds. and
concludes that they ar~ either Tertiary in age
or th~t they owe theIr present position to
thrustrng. In the summary of his work in the
General Report for the year 1920 there occurs the following statement:"The relationship between the Madhan
slates and the overlying limestone
and s.late series has not yet been
e~tabhshed. The latter, from its position, looks as if it must coincide with
Mr. R. D. Oldham's 'Infra-Blaini
beds or Simla slates'. The passage
from the Madhan slates into these
overlying beds showed no physical
discordance. but conformability between the two would necessitate a
revolution in our ideas of Simla
geology, making the rock around
Simla itself Tertiary in age. The alternative view is that ·the boundary
between the two series is a discordant one-a thrust fault in fact of
considerable magnitude, warped perhaps by subsequent movement".

In 1927. Dr. G. E. Pilgrim. in the cour~e
of a ioint survey in asG. E. Pilgrim
sociation with the present writer. spent a
few days in this area. Pilgrim identified Palmer's Mule Track series as being Lower
Chails (Purana), and concluded therefore that
the base of this series was a thrust in order
to account for the superposition of Purana
rocks on Sub'athu (Tertiary) beds. Pilgrim also
found further outcrops. of Subathu beds to

7G

APPENDICES

the west of those discovered by Hayden and
Palmer, on the south-west slopes of the Shali
ridge. His modification and extension of Palmer's map was included in the map published
with our joint memoir. Based as it was on a
visit of a few days only, it could only be re,larded as of a reconnaissance nature, though
his experience of other parts of the Simla hills
enabled him to understand certain problems
which Palmer had, perforce, to leave unsolved.
Neither Pilgrim nor Palmer, however, reaUy
appreciated the full complexity .of the str~ICture of this area, an understandllig of whIch
has only been obtained by detailed mapping
on the scale of two inches to one mile.
The dominant features of the topography
of this area are: (1) the
Topography
ShaH ridge, a branch spur
running W.S.W. from the
main .Narkanda-Fagu ridge (the ~atcrshed
between the Sutlej and Ganges dramage systems); and (2) the Sutlej river, flowing rather
south of west, north of the ShaH ridge. J:.. less
important but still well marked feat.ur~ IS the
Nauti khad, which bounds the Shah ndge on
its south side, and separates it from the .Nar-.
kanda-Fagu ridge, joining the Sutle]. at
Chaba. North of the Sutlej the ground ns~s
steadily up through Suket State to the m~m
granite ridge of Mandi State and KuIu, w?lch
a spur running W.S.W. from the great HImalaya Range, and reaches heights of 11,000
feet.
The main features of the topography are
shown in the following sketch map:The area described in this paper is shown
within the dotted lines.
The topography of the Shali area _is. one of
great relief. The summit of the Shalt IS 9,406

is

feet high. To the north of it, at a distance of
only five miles, the Sutlej river flows at 2,300
feet, while to the south of it is the Nauti khad
at about 4,400 feet.
The Shali peak forms a prominent feature
in the middle distance in the view north-east
from Simla. To anyone familiar only with this
view of the mountain, a bare dip-slope of
massive limestone, it would probabJy come as
a surprise to learn that the northern slopes are
thickly covered with a dense forest of deodar,
oak and pine. This, however, is a common
fea.ture of Himalayan ridges aligned in an
E.-W. direction, due to the northern slopes
retaining the snow and moisture for a longer
period than the southern slopes, and thus
affording the vegetati(Jn more favourable conditions for growth. Medlicott has described
the area. north of the Shali as follows:"Massive bands of limestone are tossed
about in every direction, the crumpled slate rocks have yielded easily
to denuding forces; thus producing
a combination of deep narrow gorges and of lofty rock cliffs, which are
densely covered with forest on every
available spot".

3. THE STRUCTURE IN OUTLINE

The outstanding feature of the structure of
this area, and the one
The Shali Thrust.
that gives to it such
absorbing interest. is
the ShaH thrust. The trace of this thrust describes more than three-quarters of a circle, and
is conveniently shown by a red line on the
half-inch map fOlfl11ing plate 5 of this paper.
Within the area bounded by the thrust the
general structure is that of a dome, with the
rocks dipping out-wards in all directions, as
s~own in the following
sketch-map, which
gIVes the course of the thrust and the dip of
the rocks beneath it.
The centre of this dome is not the ShaH
peak, but some distance north of it, in
The Dome-like Disposition
fact over the Sutle]·
of the Strata.
valley where the lowest
beds
appear.
This
divergence of dip is
well shown by the Upper Shali limestone
~long the Shali ridge. At the ShaH peak the dip
IS due south: further east by Nag Tikar it is
almost due east; while to the west, by Chaba,
the same rocks -are dipping south-west. The
gradual change in dip is shown best of all
by th~ overlying Chail series. which through.
out dIsplay a very regular disposition, as of
a gently warped sheet. At Chaba the dip is
south-west; below Mashobra it is south; by
Nag Tikar it is east: by Kangar it is north·
east; where it crosses the Sutlej it is northnorth-east; while westwards from here it becomes due north and continue~ thus for a long
way. Though I have deSCrIbed the general
s!ructure a-s a dome, this is not strictly correct,
Slllce the western end remains open. Nor can
it quite be described as an anticline. pitching
eastwards. It is really an anticline with the
eastern end dome-like.
Medlicott fully appreciated this steady divergence o! dip, and recognised the difficulty of
accountmg for the centre of the dome being
occupied by what he judged to be the youngest
rocks according to their grade of metamorphism. It is worth while in this connection
making two quotations from his memoir. On
page 49 he writes:"In going from Shali to the south, to the
east. or to the north. we find the
same rocks haye assumed a steadily
diverging dip".

Further on, on page 51, he writes:"Here we have a three sided divergence

trough of older strata, is more than
I can at prese·nt explain. If the east
end of this trough were an ordinary
fault, or a rapid cievation and truncation of the calcareous strata, the
case were comparatively simple; but
it is not so; the abnormal superposition of the older strata is as regular
on the east as on the north".
For an explanation he is forced to fall back
upon the possibility of
its being some kind of
MedJicott unable to solve
fan structure. an exthe structure.
planation which he rejects; or that it is an
example of the deposition of younger rocks against a steep cliff of
older rocks, a favourite explanation of his in
the C<ise of th~ iny~rsion of strata in the Ter-

tiary ro<;ks of the. outer Himalaya. Since, however, thl~ would mvolve a very extensive unconformity of the two series, he rejects this
explanation also, and is forced to leave the

The detailed mapping of the past few years
has now supplied an
answer to the problem.
Recognition of the ShaH
Th

b
h
thrust provides a solution.
e r e s a ove t e
Shali thrust· belong to
the Chail series, presumed to be Purana in
age. Those below the thrust are a mixed assemblage consisting of the Shali series, the
Madhan slates. and Subathu and Dagshai
beds of Tertiary age. As a result of nearly
horizontal movement. the Chail series have
been forced many mBes to the south-west over
strata that are younger in age, so as to overlie them as a flat sheet. Subsequent denudation
has worn away this covering of older rocks
in the Shali area, and in the "window" thus
opened we can see the younger rocks beneath.
Thus. before the denudation of the Sutlej and
the Nauti valleys, the Chail series must once
have extended~ right over what is now the
